2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVI, titled 'List of proposed conditions and recommendations'.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's review

## 2.1 Project background

3. Nearly 3 billion people worldwide use solid fuels, such as firewood and charcoal, as the primary fuel for cooking. Burning solid fuels releases emissions of carbon dioxide, methane and black carbon, which are some of the most significant contributors to global climate change. The global potential for greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reductions from the transition to improved cookstoves (ICS) is estimated at 0.6 -2.4 gigatonnes (Gt) of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) per year.
4. The proposed global project is aimed at demonstrating an innovative ICS market transformation strategy, starting in two countries (Kenya and Senegal). By doing so, it aims to improve global knowledge about the contribution of the ICS sector to nationally determined contributions (NDCs). It will significantly increase the number of ICS users in rural and most vulnerable populations in both countries, directly benefitting 11.23  million people and 1.91 million vulnerable rural households, including 0.61 million women-headed households and 5.57 million children.
5. The project will significantly limit the consumption of non-renewable biomass in the cooking sector compared to the baseline situation, leading to GHG emissions reductions of 6.47 metric tonnes (Mt) CO2eq during the project period and an additional 24.77 MtCO2eq by 2030. It will enable Senegal to reach its stated NDC target for GHG emissions reductions in cooking sector and make a substantial contribution  to the NDC GHG emissions reduction target in the cooking sector of Kenya.
6. The project aims to accelerate the growth of the ICS sector, in particular in more remote and rural locations, through an irreversible market transformation. It will transform the sector from one dominated by a number of small, artisanal, under-capitalized and informal ICS producers into a much stronger economic sector with a sufficient technological basis and business management capacities, access to commercial capital and ability to deliver better quality products to a greater number of consumers, particularly those in remote rural areas. The intended outcome of the project is to triple annual ICS production and sales volume by the end of the five-year project and achieve a six-fold increase by 2030, which is the scale required for both countries to reach their ICS-related NDC targets and achieve growth independent of official development assistance.
7. The total project costs are estimated at EUR 57.17 million (USD 65 million), including a EUR 38.36 million (USD 43.54 million) GCF grant in two tranches (tranche 1: EUR 16.51 million and tranche 2: EUR 22.85 million); EUR 12.8 million (USD 14.53 million) from the German

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.22/10/Add.05

Page 132

- Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ); and EUR 6.03 million (USD 7 million) in co-financing from a range of project partners. This range includes contributions from the national governments (Kenya: Ministry of Energy -EUR 2.4 million (USD 2.72 million) and Ministry of Health -EUR 0.5 million (USD 0.57 million); Senegal: Ministère du Pétrole et des Énergies - EUR 0.19 million (USD 0.22 million) and Ministère de l'Environnement et du Développement Durable - EUR 0.43 million (USD 0.49 million) as well as from other executing entities (EEs) and implementing partners. The co-financing ratio for GCF funds is 1:0.5.

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

8. The project consists of two national-level outputs and one global output (excluding project management):
2. (a) Output 1: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Kenya (GCF finance: EUR 19.91 million; co-financing: EUR 9.75 million) for both tranches; (b) Output 2: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Senegal (GCF finance: EUR 17.49 million; co-financing: EUR 7.46 million) for both tranches; and
3. (c) Output 3: Sharing and replication of knowledge on climate-friendly cooking solutions and their contribution to NDCs of relevant countries (GCF finance: EUR 0.95 million; cofinancing: EUR 1.61 million.

## Component 1: Strengthening the ICS supply and delivery chain in Kenya and Senegal

9. This subcomponent will address the key barriers to professionalizing and commercializing the ICS sector (see barriers 1.1 to 1.3 in section C.2 of the funding proposal). To do so, the project will provide technical assistance and limited financial support packages to qualified ICS supply chain stakeholders to: (a) improve their professionalization level; (b) expand ICS distribution and retail chains; and (c) facilitate access to market-based finance and other forms of capital for larger business-class producers. The scheme will entail the provision of tailored assistance packages, i.e. 'professionalization kits'.
10. The project will implement a performance-based support scheme to enable artisanal and professional producers to significantly increase their production and sales levels and move one class up, i.e. from artisanal to professional or from professional to business class level.
11. The kits will be provided in tranches in line with the agreed business plan, results-based criteria and milestones. The provision of tranches will be linked to and based on actual achieved sales volumes, and, in the case of business-class producers, linked to business plans.

## Component 2: Enhancing consumer demand and the ICS market environment

12. In both countries, under the second component, demand-side interventions will address barriers to consumer demand (see barriers 2.1 to 2.3 in section C.2 of the funding proposal) and market environment barriers (see barrier 2.4). Awareness is key for behavioural change in this very traditional sector. Demand for ICS is likely to be influenced by a better understanding of the risks of traditional ways of cooking, like respiratory health, safety, and convenience, among others. Technical assistance will support awareness-raising and public relations for the entire market. Awareness-raising campaigns with nationwide outreach as well as local and regional events shall sensitize the population and disseminate messages on behavioural change.
13. This subcomponent will also help finalize and streamline the institutional and policy frameworks governing the clean cooking sector, improve sector coordination and develop roadmaps and monitoring systems for sectoral mitigation targets. In addition, it will assist countries in developing and strengthening quality assurance frameworks, including capacities

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.22/10/Add.05

Page 133

- for cookstove/fuel testing, which will provide transparent data on cookstove/fuel performance in local conditions and will drive the demand towards cleaner and more efficient solutions.
- Component 3: ICS market monitoring and impact evaluation
14. The project will undertake regular ICS market monitoring during and after the completion of the GCF project to collect data and record progress made in the market transformation. The accredited entity (AE), Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ), has designed and successfully applied a thorough monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) system through its previous Energising Development (hereinafter 'EnDev') work in ICS. The MRV system is designed to provide reliable sales data and track progress at project level, including through regular meetings and consultations with producers, last mile entrepreneurs (LMEs) and users. The MRV system only reports ICS sales (which show that consumers are taking up the product), not production.
15. Periodic impact and outcome studies will be conducted including baseline assessments on the quality of the cooking systems, including health aspects.  The assessment will aim to ensure household cookstove exposure levels are tested.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

## 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

16. The proposed project aims to create a self-sustainable market for ICS, leading to substantial climate change mitigation impacts (i.e. direct project lifetime GHG emissions reductions of 6.47 MtCO2eq during the project period and an additional 24.77 MtCO2eq during the project influence period until 2030, or a cumulative 31.24 MtCO2eq over the project's lifespan) .
17. Cumulative GHG emissions reduction impact: the project will result in large and highly cost-effective direct GHG emissions reductions because of the accelerated adoption of efficient ICS during the project lifetime. It is projected that because of GCF support interventions, annual ICS sales growth rates will increase from 1.5 per cent to 24 per cent in Senegal and from 5 per cent to 36 per cent in Kenya, resulting in additional sales of 2.87 million ICS during the five years of the project duration and corresponding to 6.47 MtCO2eq in GHG emissions reductions.
18. In addition, due to the market-based approach, the ICS sector will continue to grow beyond the implementation period (albeit at a slower growth rate but higher than in the baseline scenario), leading to estimated additional sales of 11.08 million ICS and indirect GHG emissions reductions of 24.77 MtCO2eq until 2030.
19. It will also directly benefit 11.23 million people and 1.91 million vulnerable rural households, including 0.61 million women-headed households and 5.57 million children.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

20. The paradigm shift resulting from the project will facilitate a substantial contribution to the national climate goals to reduce GHG emissions in the cooking sector through the adoption of ICS by an additional 50 per cent of Senegalese households and 36 per cent of Kenyan households by 2030. It will be achieved as a result of the market transformation of the ICS sector through:
2. (a) Establishment of a new class of ICS producers that are independent of official development assistance, investment-ready, with sufficient technological base,

<!-- image -->

- managerial capacity, working capital and access to financing to sustain high ICS market growth and expansion beyond the GCF project timeframe; and
- (b) Acceleration of ICS market growth rates to reach national climate targets by 2030. The project will result in a fivefold increase in annual sales of ICS (across both countries) compared to annual sales at the start of the project in 2020, and, by doing so, it will effectively enable countries to achieve their stated NDC targets.
21. Key indicators that illustrate the intended paradigm shift can be summarized as follows:
- (a) Kenya: increase in the number of producers in the professional category from 26 to 60 and in the business category from 2 to 20, leading to an increase in annual ICS sales from a projected 252,000 ICS at the beginning of 2020 up to 1,116,621 ICS by project end, and up to 1,978,162 ICS by 2030; and
- (b) Senegal: increase in the number of producers in the professional category from 25 to 60 and in the business category from 3 to 25, leading to an increase in annual ICS sales from a projected 203,000 ICS at the beginning of 2020 up to 586,325  ICS by project end, and up to 1,096,671 ICS by 2030.
22. The replication potential of the project within the targeted countries is significant. The unserved market for ICS now includes more than 6 million households (more than 5 million in Kenya and about 1.4 million in Senegal). While the proposed project aims to tap this considerable potential (ICS sales will grow during the project lifetime by 24 per cent and 36 per cent per year in Senegal and Kenya, respectively), it will also create conditions for sustained ICS sector growth beyond its timeframe, thus ensuring the continued replication impact of the GCF investment. The replication potential in other counties is also significant. Globally there are over three billion people still relying on inefficient biomass use for cooking, and the number is steadily growing due to population growth. Knowledge about the climate benefits of ICS remains limited, thus the importance of this sector in achieving global and national climate goals is also not widely perceived. The project will contribute to improving knowledge about the ICS sector and its integration in NDCs in at least two further countries with a high share of GHG emissions from the cooking sector.
23. The creation of knowledge among the many ICS sector stakeholders, producers, distributors, individual consumers, and community organisations, as well as public authorities is a key element of the ICS market transformation strategy of the project. Among others, the project will provide extensive training and tailored mentorship to some 100 ICS producers to help improve their knowledge about international best practices regarding ICS manufacturing and product design. It will also provide training to 2,000 additional LMEs on effective marketing and sales approach for ICS. The project will support collaboration with universities and public offices involved in testing and labelling ICS, will support national and local government in allocating resources for necessary public sector support measures for testing, labelling and communication of benefits, and will work with national and local stakeholders on designing effective promotion campaigns for ICS.
24. The project is also aimed at creating an enabling environment for accelerated ICS market growth in Kenya and Senegal. The core elements of such an enabling environment include a strong ICS production sector, an effective network of distributors and retailers, a group of experienced local NGOs for the support of the ICS sector, strong national alliances, interministerial cooperation, and a broad awareness on the benefits and viability of ICS usage amongst all relevant population groups in the two countries.
25. The project will contribute to regulatory framework and policies. In Kenya, the project will support the Energy Regulatory Commission (ERC) and Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS) in finalisation, adoption, and enforcement of ICS regulations and standards, as well as national ICS quality labels. In Senegal, the project will support the development of an investment plan for

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.22/10/Add.05

the domestic energy strategy, including estimation of the investment needs and potential public, private, and international sources to finance its implementation.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

26. The project will deliver numerous sustainable development benefits and make a tangible contribution to a number of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) for 11.23 million people directly during the project lifetime and over 30.16 million people indirectly by 2030 via:
2. (a) Reduction of energy poverty (SDG 1);
3. (b) Lessening exposure to health risks (SDG 3);
4. (c) Reducing drudgery for women in collecting wood fuel (SDG 5);
5. (d) Improving access to modern energy (SDG 7);
6. (e) Reducing GHG emissions and strengthening communities' resilience to climate change (SDG 13); and
7. (f) Diminishing pressure on forest resources (SDG 15)
27. The project will also result in the following additional climate change adaptation benefits:
9. (a) Reductions in fuel use directly contribute to the saving of resources (money or labour) required by the households to acquire the fuel. These avoided expenditures can allow households to allocate the resources into investments for adaptation measures;
10. (b) Climate change in Senegal and Kenya is increasing desertification and reducing the availability of biomass fuel. Reductions in specific fuel use allows households to adapt to shortages in the supply of biomass fuel; and
11. (c) Potentially improved health outcomes for families adopting the ICS technology can lead to an improved level of resources for adaptation.  The household level emissions will need to be monitored and field, as well as laboratory, testing of the stoves to ensure that the health benefits are realised.
28. The project will also benefit vulnerable groups. The project has been designed to maximize and ensure the long-term sustainability of its climate impacts through a national market transformation. Yet at the same time, this transformation will ensure substantial adaptation co-benefits for the most vulnerable groups of consumers. In Senegal, the underserved targeted by the project have also been identified as among the most vulnerable to climate impacts in view of their climate risk profile and dependence depleting forest resources.
29. Scaling up ICS adoption also reduces the pressure on forests and biodiversity, reduces drudgery for women in collecting wood fuel, and saves money if fuel needs to be purchased. Benefits from the wide-scale adoption of improved cookstoves outweigh their costs sevenfold if and when the health, social and environmental benefits are accounted for.
30. Further measures to improve the supply of biomass fuel and forestry management outside of the proposed project in both countries are also necessary to adequately address the problems resulting from the growing demand for biomass fuel.
31. Other sustainable development benefits of the project include the reduction of energy poverty, creation of new long-term jobs, and additional educational opportunities and incomegenerating activities, in particular for women engaged in the ICS value chain.

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.22/10/Add.05

Page 136

32. Participating countries are low-income to lower-middle-income countries that have demonstrated commitments to promoting ICS, including via their NDCs, but lack sufficient implementation and financial capacities to carry out comprehensive market transformation programmes without additional international support. This project specifically targets poor, vulnerable and marginalized populations that lack access to the ICS market.
33. Senegal is one of the world's least developed countries; it is ranked 162 out of 188 in the Human Development Index. Senegal suffers from a persistently high poverty rate, which is currently 46.7 per cent. Overall, 17 per cent of people are food insecure, and in some (mostly rural) parts of the country the prevalence of global acute malnutrition is critical. Rapid population growth (2.9 per cent/year) will further exacerbate competition for and pressure on natural resources as well as vulnerability to climate change. Almost all of the 1.6 million households in Senegal use one, or, for the most part, two stoves for firewood or charcoal to prepare their daily food. EnDev estimates that about 87 per cent of households still rely on inefficient cookstoves.
34. Kenya's population is estimated at 48 million (about 9.6 million households according to 2016 data) and is growing at an annual average rate of 2.7 per cent. Seventy-eight per cent of the population live in rural regions and 22 per cent live in urban areas. Thirty-three per cent of urban dwellers and 51 per cent of people in rural areas (45 per cent of the population) live below the poverty line on less than USD 2 a day. The country is resource poor and vulnerable to external and economic shocks from rising oil costs, security concerns and climatic conditions. The Government of Kenya is committed to reducing its fiscal deficit to less than 6 per cent of gross domestic product from its current level at over 8 per cent through a combination of costcutting and revenue-enhancing measures. Increasing the uptake of resource efficiency via clean cooking would provide a small buffer for this fiscal anomaly.

## 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

35. The project has been designed in close alignment with the national climate and development priorities of the targeted countries, as presented in the following key national policy and strategy documents.
36. Kenya's intended nationally determined contribution has also identified clean energy technologies (including ICS) as a high potential intervention area for achieving the country's mitigation and adaptation objectives. The NDC further resulted in a number of specific plans and policies. Kenya has developed a National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS 2010), National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP 2013) and a National Adaptation Plan (NAP 2016), which provide the vision for low-carbon and climate-resilient development, as well as the National Climate Change Framework Policy and legislation.
37. The climate change actions target different areas, such as afforestation and reforestation, geothermal and other clean energy development, energy efficiency, climate-smart agriculture and drought management. Specifically, the draft NCCAP for 2018-2022, which is still under revision, emphasizes, inter alia, the strategic importance of the following measures aimed at expedited ICS market development in Kenya:
4. (a) Promotion of local manufacture, development and enforcement of quality standards and servicing of clean cookstoves, for example, through tax-relief incentives for manufacturers, training (both management and technical), loans and other required support for local service providers;

(b) Support for local businesses to stock ICS, with an emphasis on women-led businesses; and

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.22/10/Add.05

Page 137

- (c) Establishment of timber plantations for sustainable biomass supply through various programmes such as agroforestry.
38. In Senegal, because of the large share of CO2 emissions stemming from the use of firewood and charcoal for cooking, there are explicit interventions for reducing the use of nonrenewable wood as fuel for domestic consumption defined in the NDC. Ambitious targets for interventions addressing the emissions from domestic cooking with wood fuels have been defined in the mitigation objectives in the NDC, including specifically the diffusion of 8.4 million ICS for firewood and charcoal. As far as Senegal's key development priorities are concerned, the project will directly contribute to the two strategic priorities of the Government of Senegal, as formulated in the Plan Sénégal Emergent (PSE) to (i) foster economic growth and (ii) reduce poverty. To do so, the International Monetary Fund notes in its recent Fifth Review under the Policy Support Instrument (December 2017) that 'further progress is needed on implementing measures to facilitate SME access to capital and, more broadly, the transition of the informal sector to the formal sector'. By focusing on the professionalization and commercialization of the largely informal ICS sector in the country, the project will make a direct contribution to this national policy priority.
39. The GCF project will be embedded into on-going GIZ programmes in Kenya and Senegal where the core project implementation structure is already in place. The programme has a very good track record of stakeholder engagement. They regularly consult with project partners, institutions and NGOs. Project teams also exert a very close monitoring of beneficiaries and of supported ICS producers, usually in cooperation with grassroot level organisations, such as the Agricultural Services in Kenya who meet every  months with LMEs to monitor their sales. This practice of regular stakeholder engagement has been used during preparation of the GCF proposal.

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium/High

40. The proposed GCF project has been designed to ensure minimum concessionality of GCF funding such that GCF grant funding is deployed only to support viable climate initiatives that cannot currently be financed by the non-grant instruments.
41. Without GCF support the financial viability of the four ICS producer classes is poor. Cash-based, informal activity will lead to underinvestment in equipment. The support the producers do receive tends to be directed towards increasing marketing and distribution actions as opposed to production. It does not allow for hiring additional staff, creating sufficient stock or increasing productivity. It is essentially a stagnant situation, with an increase in sales of 5 per cent per year (Kenya) and 1.5 per cent per year (Senegal) and limited capital investment.
42. For Kenya, the average financial internal rate of return (FIRR) is between 5 per cent and 2 per cent, which indicates very modest returns. In Senegal, capital investment in the artisan category is positive in the business-as-usual (BAU) case. For the professional class, there is a destruction of value since the BAU anticipates general underinvestment and postponing of significant marketing and distribution activities. Only necessary investments in electric and manual machines are continued (at lower amounts). Investments in safety equipment, work wear and tools are either completely eliminated or curtailed significantly. With sales increased by only 1.5 per cent per year over the baseline, the BAU scenario for this category is -12 per cent. Outside support for investment is clearly needed in order to reach the next business category.
43. With GCF support, the financial viability of the four ICS producer classes improves with the introduction of GCF financing. The results range from an FIRR of 9 per cent to 19 per cent. If the designated capital expenditures are implemented and additional staff hired, then production increases as does the extent of the marketing and distribution network. It is important to note

<!-- image -->

Page 138

- that, though these FIRR results are positive, they are modest and do not allow the ICS producers sufficient returns to access the respective traditional corporate banking markets from a purely financial point of view. This sets the stage for the technical assistance and professionalization activities that will improve the ICS producers, increase productivity, and lead to a subset of producers that are able to formulate business plans and successfully conclude financing agreements with financing entities in the respective markets.
44. A sensitivity analysis on the 'with GCF support' scenario on the key investment parameters at the ICS producer level in Kenya and Senegal was carried out. The following observations can be made concerning the sensitivity analysis:
- (a) Generally, the FIRR estimates are more volatile to changes in different inputs. This is because there are fewer variables driving these calculations. The FIRR calculation relies on four inputs (capex, sales revenue, cost of production and cost of sales) while taxes are minor and derived from these inputs;
- (b) FIRR calculations are most sensitive to changes in sales and costs of production. Variations in capital expenditures do not have such a strong impact on the FIRR calculation. The most significant sensitivity impact is the combination of a decrease in sales and increase in capital expenditures (negative outlook); and
- (c) Economic internal rate of return (EIRR) estimates are not as volatile to changes in different inputs. This is partly due to more factors (the addition of the economic value of GHG emissions reductions) than the four inputs used in the FIRR calculations. In fact, additional economic benefits from GHG emissions reductions are very large and are either equal to or outweigh most of the other factors in value.

Given the result of the sensitivity analysis and the impact on FIRR and EIRR for the sector, the increasing scale and sales volumes has a huge impact on the sustainability of the market.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

## 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

45. Environmental and social risk category. The AE did not identify any significant adverse environmental and social risks and impacts of the project. The direct impacts are expected to be minor and would come mainly from the project's support for small-scale manufacturing operations or from 'professional' and 'business class' ICS producers. The project was screened by the AE through its Safeguards and Gender management system as Category B taking into consideration the geographical extent and implementation arrangements.
46. Environmental and social assessment. The accredited entity has provided an Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA). The ESA which included an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) that identifies and addresses the potential unintended negative environmental impacts and risks of ICS production. The ESMP presents the organization and staffing for safeguards, tasks and responsibilities, grievance redress mechanism, occupational health and safety standards as part of the 'professionalization kits' and a checklist for the contents of individual producer's ESMP. The ESA also provides a brief description of the national environmental and social legislation in Kenya and Senegal relevant to the project. It is expected that environmental permits will be required for the business class production.
47. Environmental and social risks and impacts. The project proposal emphasized that aside from reduced GHG emissions, the beneficial impacts of the project would include: (1) more efficient use of fuelwoods; and (2) lower emission of particulate matter which likely also improves the indoor air quality of homes. The AE was able to identify and describe other wider

<!-- image -->

- benefits from the project that include reduction of health risks, reduction of pressure on forest and biodiversity, reduction of energy poverty and provision of long-term opportunities such as jobs and income-generating activities.
48. The direct environmental and social risks would include occupational health and safety, material sourcing (i.e., sand and clay extraction), industrial pollution and nuisance due to use of energy and operation of mechanized equipment and labor conditions. Land and land rights acquisitions, if any, by the producers are expected to be private (i.e., willing buyer-willing seller) transaction hence no displacement or involuntary resettlement impacts are expected. There are indirect risks associated with the development of the ICS industry and market which need to be addressed in the project design. In addition, the AE triggered its conflict and context sensitivity safeguard for activities in Kenya, requiring analysis of the conflict potential and context sensitivity. Measures to avoid risks are included in the activity designs.
49. Indigenous peoples. The project is not expected to adversely affect the indigenous peoples or ethnic communities. Instead, the project will bring about positive impacts as it will contribute to resource conservation and reduce potential conflicts over resources. The ESMP provides that the project and stakeholder engagement will be implemented in a culturallyappropriate manner. A code of conduct will be adopted for all implementing partners staff to ensure that the project activities will be carried out using culturally-appropriate and gendersensitive approaches.
50. Community health and safety. The project is seen to contribute to the improvements in the safety of cooking and of respiratory health due to the reduction of indoor air pollution from using ICS compared to replaced traditional cooking systems. End-user exposure risk, however, will need to be monitored through field testing to ensure that exposure at the household level will not get worse. The project proposes periodic studies as part of the impact evaluation that would include the quality of the cooking energy systems (CES) using the CES methodology developed through the EnDev. All the stove designs have passed the safety and emissions tests by the national laboratories. The project will also continue to work with the national certification bodies and product standard bodies to improve product health and safety of the ICS.
51. Indirect risks and impacts. There are potential indirect risks and impacts of the project across the industry. The popularity and ease of the use of ICS may lead to more fuel and therefore contribute to increasing pressure on fuelwood. This will need to be monitored through the project's impact evaluation that will regularly assess any changes in the fuelwood use in households. In addition, poor or lack of product standards for ICS could also lead to failure to maximize indoor air quality improvements, product safety, and better and safer manufacturing processes. At the level of national product certification and standard bodies, EnDev will continue to support and contribute to the improvement of standards. In parallel and as part of the project, measures to improve standards and ensure product safety include (1) improvements of manufacturing processes and standard of operations and assets; (2) use of ICS designs that have passed safety and emission tests by the national laboratories; and (3) further monitoring and impact evaluation to periodically assess indoor exposure to emissions from cooking. The indirect risks are addressed in the project design. For example, there is a mention of the provision of 'training on the use of equipment, work safety, and social standard of work'; and, 'support to achieve national product quality standards' as part of the 'professional kit' support. The project will work with other stakeholders to promote further actions for improving supply chain as well as addressing barriers related to sustainable fuelwood production and other problems related to biomass supply.
52. The Project ESMP describes a process for due diligence of ICS producers receiving support from the project. The process includes the environmental and social screening of business and/or production plans by the project environmental and social officer and the

<!-- image -->

- producers, the preparation of producer's ESMP, review, approval monitoring and audit. ESMP activities and costs are incorporated into the project.
53. The ESMP describes the various environmental and social management responsibilities of the project. The AE shall oversee compliance with its safeguards and gender management system. Overall guidance is provided by a Project Advisory Board while a Project Coordination Committees will be responsible for monitoring compliance to environmental, social and fiduciary safeguards. The Project ESMP implementation will be supported at the national level by an Environmental and Social Officer and a national focal person for gender management and at the sub-national or regional level by designated regional focal points for social management and gender mainstreaming. All the executing entities and implementing partners will also have designated focal points for environmental and social management.
54. The ESMP also provides guidelines for environmentally sound and socially responsible manufacturing practices. The guidelines consider safe working conditions, sourcing of raw materials like clay and sand, waste management, applicable national legislation and policies on occupational health and safety, labor and waste (including hazardous waste) management. Professional and business class ICS producers will be required to develop their own ESMP for their production facilities which at the minimum shall be based on the guidelines described in the ESMP.
55. The project is embedded in the ongoing EnDev programme in Kenya and Senegal and therefore benefits from established implementation arrangements and stakeholder mechanisms. In particular, in the course of developing the project, stakeholder consultations were held from 2017 to 2018 with the identified national and local stakeholder stakeholders in the two countries including sectoral ministries, cookstove sectors, women groups, private sector groups including the chamber of crafts, academe and research centers. Further stakeholder engagement activities are embedded in the project activities including external communication and dissemination.
56. Engagement at various levels is ensured through the Project Advisory Board at the national institutional level and the Project Coordination Committees at the executing entity levels. Knowledge exchange at the global level through the project's output 3 allows engagement with international stakeholders. Engagement with the communities is maintained through the projects continuing interactions with the ICS supply chain including end users.
57. Grievance redress mechanisms at various levels will be used for the project. At the project-scale, the grievance mechanism will utilize the consultation processes to collect any grievances and issues of beneficiaries, producers, supply chain workers, and project staff. At the production-level, business-class ICS producers will develop their own local mechanism. At the programme-level, EnDev country teams will also be able to accept written complaints.

## 4.2 Gender policy

58. The proposal contains a comprehensive and thorough gender assessment, and therefore complies with the operational guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. It also includes a gender action plan to support the implementation of issues identified in the gender assessment.
59. The gender assessment indicates that both Senegal and Kenya are countries with gender equality issues, including low scores in economic participation, educational attainment and political empowerment (though Senegal has surpassed the world average, with 47 per cent of women in its parliament). There are laws and legislations that support the equality of women and men in access to land, resources and assets, but customary laws and social norms still need to be addressed to achieve gender equality in the counties. Both countries have policies that try

<!-- image -->

- to promote gender equality, but implementation and capacity to implement is still a challenge for both countries.
60. The gender assessment of the sector for both countries provides in-depth information on the cooking sector. The assessment was done based on reviews of studies and consultation with women's organizations in both Kenya and Senegal. Energy-efficient ICS have a potential to reduce exposure to smoke-related health hazards, ease the burden on women and children to collect fuelwood, and help the poor save money (when the fuel needs to be purchased). The assessment recognizes that women face the brunt of the problem but are also the potential solution holders through the purchase and use of ICS as well as in engaging in the production of ICS. Findings indicate that women are found to be efficient in selling cookstoves and much more efficient if provided with the same level of training as men. However, the assessment indicates that women's household work is a barrier to spending much more time engaging in the production of ICS unless some female relatives perform these household tasks on their behalf. The consultation held with women demonstrated that women are still less confident than men to engage in entrepreneurship activities and that even though women are found to be decision makers with regard to domestic purchases, they do not seem to have capital at their disposal or in some cases have poor decision-making power. There is also the issue of women having less confidence than men in administrative matters and are found to be less literate than men (particularly in Senegal) in terms of writing and record-keeping, which is a limitation in their ability to access financing and other services.
61. The project understands that there will be gender-related issues that need to be addressed but will tread very carefully, meaning it will use a gender-sensitive approach, to address changing gender roles between men and women. The findings from the consultations with women also indicated that the roles played by women and men do not pose any challenge so far. Therefore, the recommended approach by the project is to focus on interventions with men aimed at convincing them of the benefits of cookstove use, such as the potential reduction in time spent by women on cooking. Investment will contribute to facilitating equal opportunities for women and men to access income-generating activities across the ICS value chain. The project will strengthen work with women groups, as experience to date indicates success. The project will invest in building institutional gender mainstreaming capacities and the monitoring of interventions to ensure no negative impacts come up as a result of the intervention. The assessment also indicates that female heads of households will not face any disadvantages and will benefit equally from the interventions. The AE also intends to, maybe not directly but through its connections with other non-governmental organizations (NGOs) that will participate in the project as EEs or service providers, have a focus on gender work and thus leverage gender support through these cooperation partners outside of the GCF-financed interventions, such as changes in the division of labour and childcare as well as changes in traditional roles.
62. The project is expected to have major positive impacts on addressing challenges faced by women by ensuring access to and use of ICS to reduce the burden in collecting fuel wood and provide women with income-earning opportunities through engagement in the ICS value chain activities. The assessment has identified that there are opportunities for work/employment for people with disabilities as well.
63. The action plan provides the corresponding actions to address the issues identified. The logic model in the funding proposal provides the budgets, targets for women and youth, and separate indicators for the two countries.
64. A GIZ gender focal point will be appointed for each country and each region/country of intervention to coordinate the implementation of the gender action plan.

## 4.3 Risks

<!-- image -->

65. Overall programme assessment (medium risk):

(a) GCF is requested to provide a grant of EUR 38.3 million for the provision of technical assistance to strengthen the ICS supply chain. The total project cost is EUR 57.5 million with co-financing by way of grants from  a range ofinstitutions including BMZ, the Governments of Kenya and Senegal, and implementing partners. The project is expected to leverage parallel financing of EUR 3.17 million from ICS producers, banks, financing partners and ICS consumers; and

(b) The project is based on the pilot approach of and experience gained through EnDev. Over 12 years, EnDev supported the ICS sector in moving from the pre-commercial towards the pioneering stage. The project success depends on the ability of AEs/EEs and implementing partners to convince unserved households to invest in ICS as well as the capacity of the ICS producers to accelerate the scaling up process at the expected rate.

66. AE/EE capability to execute the current programme (medium risk):

(a) GIZ (the AE) has an extensive track record in implementing ICS over 12 years in the region. The AE also acts as one of the EEs. GIZ currently operates in 25 countries in Africa, Latin America and Asia under the global EnDev energy access partnership, including 19 countries with active ICS support projects. In all components, GIZ will take the responsibility of sector coordination, oversight and supervision. The funding proposal states that GIZ will ensure a clear and strict separation between the two different roles as an AE and EE by different GIZ units assuming the roles;

- (b) In Kenya, the project is implemented by a government EE (the Ministry of Energy) and two international EEs (GIZ Kenya and SNV Netherlands Development Organisation). In Senegal, the project will be implemented by GIZ Senegal as a leading EE. In addition, three large NGOs (ENDA Energie, enda ECOPOP and ONG CONCEPT Senegal) will be implementing awareness-raising and last-mile marketing support on the ground in 15,000 villages in the 14 regions in the country. The three NGOs have been selected via a competitive expression of interest, which included a due diligence process to determine management and administrative capacities and physical presence in the regions; and
- (c) As identified in the funding proposal, the project involves multiple EEs and other implementation partners for the activities on the ground, which makes the implementation structure complicated. The AE is relied upon to provide necessary capacity-building support when required and minimize the administrative and transaction costs during the implementation of the project.

67. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk):

- (a) Complaints from dropout artisanal producers: the funding proposal expects that approximately 20 small artisanal producers will drop out due to the limitation in growth potential during the commercialization process. There is a risk of provoking backlash from the dropout producers, and a grievance mechanism is required to properly handle the complaints during the implementation of the project. The AE states that the project teams have a good track record of regularly consulting beneficiaries, and a triple grievance mechanism will be established;

(b) Delay in execution: there are three potential cases that may cause delays in execution: (1) slow start-up at the initial phase of the project; 2) ICS producers not being able to scale up at the anticipated rate; and (3) producers not being able to make 20 per cent of their own contribution in full up front. In the event of delay in execution, it will affect the level of impact of the project. The disbursement is not linked to a fixed calendar schedule, although the project expects that the initial start-up investment will be completed by the end of year 2, quarter eight. In case a significant number of producers do not qualify for the first tranche within the first two years, the AE states that the

<!-- image -->

project will assess the sales growth and future performance and consider whether it will allow for a grace period to reach the targeted sales volume or reallocate the support. In addition, if the 20 per cent producer contribution proves to a be a large hurdle in the first year of implementation, the project will assess the performance/potential growth and consider either delaying the disbursement until the 20 per cent contribution is made or allowing an instalment plan for the producer contribution. The detailed criteria for options to consider will be elaborated in the grant operation manual. Components 1.1.1 and 2.1.1 include the provision for performance-based incentives. Given this nature of the project, it is recommended that the GCF disbursement be proportionate with the co-financing amount for the performance-based grants and undisbursed funds for component 1.1.1 and 2.1.1 to be returned to GCF;

(c) Low willingness to pay in rural areas: the funding proposal states that there is great market potential for ICS, but actual demand is geographically limited. The demand is mostly based in urban and peri-urban areas for both countries, and the project focuses more on rural areas. The funding proposal estimates the latent demand potential by calculating the number of unserved households (6.7 million) and the percentage of the households (70 -90 per cent) who are able to pay. The funding proposal indicates that globally, more than 85 per cent of the population could purchase a basic ICS at a price of around USD 5. However, there is a gap between ability to pay and willingness to pay, and the project is planning to reach out to an expanded geographical region to overcome the barrier of low willingness to pay and mobilize rural sales at a significant rate. The project aims to deploy various methods to sensitize the population in rural areas based on the research and the result from a pilot project. The AEs/EEs are expected to closely monitor the sales volume in the rural areas; and

(d) Economic and financial viability: the funding proposal demonstrates that the cost of EUR 3.86 per household for the awareness-raising campaign for sensitizing (activities 1.2.1 and 2.2.1) is cost efficient compared to the range of global estimates (USD 1 -9 per household). It also shows that the financial viability of the project with GCF support resulting in an FIRR ranging from 8 per cent to 19 per cent. However, the sensitivity analysis shows that if there is a decrease in sales by 10 per cent, the FIRR could be negative even with GCF grant financing. The project aims to create a self-sustained market for ICS, leading to substantial climate change mitigation impacts.

68. GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk):

(a) If approved, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration risk remains non-material.  The project remains within the GCF risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single proposal.

69. Compliance (medium risk):

(a) This project deals with the conversion of cookstoves to more efficient versions, thereby reducing carbon emissions. The project is expected to involve some significant procurements and distribution of the improved technology among beneficiaries. Typically, these activities can encounter a relatively high level of corruption risk. Therefore, it is important that the AE and delivery partners exercise a great amount of due diligence and oversight into their procurement and financial accountability practices. The AE (GIZ) is a well-known and respected development partner and has a number of measures and safeguards in place to oversee and address these risks. Furthermore, the AE is bound by its agreements with GCF to exercise appropriate oversight and accountability over the use of GCF funds in this project. As GCF relies on GIZ and its partners to exercise the appropriate controls and oversight, the risks for corruption and related financial integrity issues should be mitigated. Accordingly, the project would be assigned a 'medium' risk rating for compliance.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

71. During project implementation, GIZ will have the dual role of an AE as well as an EE. As AE, GIZ will take oversight responsibility for the overall project and, among other functions, will administer the funds on behalf of GCF and provide oversight guidance and quality assurance through its relevant headquarter units for the EEs.
72. The AE will work with two additional EEs in Kenya, namely the Ministry of Energy and SNV Netherlands Development Organisation. In Senegal, the project will be executed by three other EEs: ENDA Energie, enda ECOPOP, ONG CONCEPT Senegal and GIZ/EnDev Senegal. In cases where the above EEs do not implement activities, additional implementation partners involved in the value chain will be identified to implement specific activities under service or financial contracts. Agreements will be concluded by the AE with all EEs in all three components of the project, whereby subsidiary agreements with the AE will be signed based on GIZ standard operating procedures.
73. The Project Governing Board will be the existing EnDev governing board structure for the overall governance of all activities. Members of the Board are representatives of BMZ, the Department for International Development of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, the Directorate-General for International Cooperation of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Netherlands, the Norwegian Agency for Development Cooperation, the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency and the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation.
74. Both Kenya and Senegal country will have a Project Advisory Board tasked with the mandate of providing overall project guidance, review and feedback, programme synergy and coherence, adherence with environmental and social safeguards and gender action plan objectives, among other things. On an operational level, project coordination committees will be

<!-- image -->

- established to ensure structured implementation of project activities across project components.
75. An operational manual will be developed for each country before project commencement to guide the implementation of a results-based support scheme. It will cover in detail the following aspects of the scheme such as eligibility criteria, key results indicators, implementation arrangements, financial management and so forth.
76. The financial management of the project will follow the internal rules and regulations of GIZ. As a general principle, GIZ disburses funds to the recipients in accordance with the progress of the project. The EEs have to prove the proper use of funds and the expected progress of the project as a prerequisite for any further disbursement.
77. In case of GIZ procurement, the AE will follow its own procurement guidelines. However, where EEs are involved, the procurement and financial management function will be subject to subsidiary agreements with GIZ, which will include adherence to GIZ standard operating procedures. Independent external auditors will perform annual financial audits of the project in line with the International Standards on Auditing.
78. It is recommended that, as a condition of funded activity agreement effectiveness, the AE shall confirm that it will deliver to the GCF a completed, detailed evaluation of the capacity of the EE and implementing partners (where applicable), indicating that they have the necessary capacity to implement the project. Furthermore, it is recommended that the AE review its AE fee request and project management cost structure in the budget to be within the revised GCF Policy on Fees for Accredited Entities and Delivery Partners,  approved by the Board at its nineteenth meeting. Additional comments on the funding proposal and detailed budget, where applicable, will be shared in due course.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

79. As a mitigation project, the intervention has a direct GHG reduction potential estimated at 6.46 million tCO2eq (5.39 million in Kenya and 1.08 million in Senegal) over the project lifespan. The expected total number of direct and indirect beneficiaries is expected to be approximately 11.23 million, reflected as per the gender-disaggregated metrics of relevant GCF impact and outcome indicators.
80. Overall the funding proposal and logframe accurately and consistently apply the GCF (impact and outcome) results management framework/performance management framework indicators, and the project has built in baselines and data collection that can inform progress reporting with credible evidence on expected results.
81. The funding proposal's theory of change in implementation will benefit from further details that reflect the causal pathways at project level and in relation to the climate rationale (which ideally can be tested in implementation either with project performance management indicators or impact data/evidence generated to attribute changes to GCF investment).
82. Regarding section H.1, overall the logic framework complies with GCF standards and has been cleared through Secretariat review.
83. Under section H.2, the Secretariat welcomes the application of impact assessment tools for data collection and recommends considering that the final independent evaluation be conducted as an impact evaluation rather than a process performance evaluation.

## 4.6 Legal assessment

<!-- image -->

84. The Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) was signed with the accredited entity on 15 November 2017, and it became effective on 18 January 2019.
85. The accredited entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the GCF Board the GCF receives a certificate or legal opinion from the accredited entity in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat confirming that all final internal approvals by the accredited entity have been obtained and that the entity has the authority and capacity to implement the project.
86. The proposed project will be implemented in Kenya and Senegal. The GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities in any of the aforementioned countries. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. The Secretariat has engaged with the relevant countries as follows:
4. (a) With respect to Kenya, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the government of Kenya on 30 September 2015. The agreement is currently under negotiation;
5. (b) With respect to Senegal, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the government of Senegal on 30 September 2015. Currently, there are no ongoing negotiations.
87. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, where appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in Kenya and Senegal, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.
88. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the GCF Board is made subject to the following conditions:
8. (a) Delivery by the accredited entity to the GCF of a certificate or legal opinion confirming
9. (b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of GCF Board approval or the date on which the accredited entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has the accredited entity, whichever is later; and

that it has obtained all its internal approvals within 120 days of the GCF Board approval, or the date effectiveness of the AMA, whichever is later; obtained all internal approvals, or the date of effectiveness of the AMA entered into with

- (c) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.
