## Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP103

| Proposal name:          | Promotion of Climate-Friendly cooking: Kenya and Senegal          |
|-------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Accredited entity:      | Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale ZusammenarbeitGmbH (GIZ) |
| Project/programme size: | Medium                                                            |

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

## 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

1. Over 40 per cent of the world's population still use solid fuels as the primary source of cooking energy. The stoves generally use inferior quality fuel (mostly biomass, fuelwood and charcoal), their burning efficiencies are poor and as a result the cooking processes not only cause emissions of greenhouse gases (GHGs) but also health hazards. Generally, women and children around them are exposed to toxic gases and black fumes. Improved cooking stoves (ICS) can potentially increase burning efficiency and thereby reduce emissions of GHGs. It is estimated that the promotion and subsequent use of ICS worldwide could reduce atmospheric emissions within the range of 0.6 to 2.4 gigatonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (GtCO2eq) per year.
2. A large majority of African households still use solid fuels in traditional cooking stoves. People living in Kenya and Senegal are no exception. So far, the penetration of ICS in these countries has been very low despite high willingness to pay among both rural and urban populations, adequate enough to pay for the market price of ICS. The root cause of the problem lies in micro-scale production and sales operation by artisanal producers of ICS and lack of capacity as well as finance in activities related to production and marketing of ICS. Overseas development assistance (ODA) does not generate adequate investment to address the financing gap. Moreover, there are needs for training and linkages with formal credit systems on the supply side and subsequent awareness-building and persuasion on the demand side to transform the sector. Even the design of available ICS units needs to be tweaked to fit countryand culture-specific criteria. Since the technologies have already been initiated, the importance of financial support appears to be paramount towards initiating a paradigm shift in Kenya and Senegal.
3. The proposed project aims to demonstrate an innovative strategy to transform the ICS market in the two target countries and, by doing so, improve global knowledge about the ICS sector contribution to nationally determined contributions (NDCs). The project intends to accelerate the growth of the ICS sector, particularly in more remote and rural locations, with an irreversible market transformation. It will transform the sector from one which is dominated by a number of small, artisanal, under-capitalized and informal ICS producers into a much stronger economic sector. This includes adequate technological know-how and capacities for business management, gaining access to commercial capital and the ability to deliver better-quality products to a greater number of consumers, particularly in remote rural areas.
4. The intended outcome of the project is to triple annual ICS production and sales volume by the project end (after five years) and achieve a roughly six fold increase by 2030. Analysis suggests that such a scale is required for both countries to substantially reach their ICS-related NDC targets and to achieve ODA-independent growth. The project will significantly increase the

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.21/10/Add.05

Page 148

- number of ICS users among the rural and most-vulnerable populations in both countries, directly benefitting 11.23 million people and 1.91 million vulnerable rural households, including 0.61 million women-headed households and 5.57 million children.
5. The project will significantly limit consumption of non-renewable biomass in the cooking sector compared to the baseline situation leading to GHG emission reductions of 6.47 MtCO2eq during the project period.
6. The total project costs are estimated at EUR 57.19 million (about USD 65 million), including a EUR 38.36 million (USD 43.54 million) GCF grant, EUR 12.8 million (USD 14.53 million) from BMZ (the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development), and EUR 6.03 million (USD 7 million) in co-financing from a range of project partners. The project will be financed in two tranches: (1) tranche 1 involves EUR 16.18 million for the activities up to the end of year 2, and (2) tranche 2 involves EUR 22.18 million to be mobilized in year 3.
7. It is expected that, owing to the market-based approach, the ICS sector will continue to grow beyond the implementation period at a rate which is higher than the baseline growth rate. The growth rate will eventually lead to an estimated additional sales of 13.946 million ICS units and cause an indirect (additional) emission reduction of 24.77 MtCO2eq until 2030. It will enable Senegal to reach its stated NDC target for GHG emissions in the energy cooking sector and make a substantial contribution (approximately 80 per cent) to the NDC GHG emission reduction target in the cooking stove sector of Kenya.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

8. The project in not a novel project, since the promotion of ICS has already been established on a limited scale in the target countries. In that respect, it is not innovative as such. However, the delivery modalities have been developed based on thorough analyses and subsequent innovation in terms of approach and organization. The sector and situation analyses in both countries have helped identify current gaps, while the project has addressed each gap and devised methods to address the gaps that will lead to targeted activities and incentives. Moreover, the provisions created, as proposed, never sought financial full concessionality to incentivize end users, but rather have resorted to enabling activities that operate through market mechanisms. In this regard, the delivery of the project has elements that are innovative and highly likely to succeed. If that happens, the current paradigm involving low investmentlow output artisanal production of ICS will be shifted towards a commercial production of ICS that meets the demand for achieving targets enshrined in the respective NDCs of the two countries, as submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention of Climate Change (UNFCCC).
9. One of the elements of paradigm shift may be realized when the current dependency on ODA for the promotion of ICS is replaced by a market-oriented demand and supply of ICS that involves both mature producers and end users. This will counter the current painstakingly slow penetration of ICS due to unreliable ODA-dependent financing and encourage the establishment of a business model that relies on market-based financing and mutual benefits, supported by awareness, regulatory frameworks and further improvements in design. This will then be a completely new paradigm for the ICS market in the two countries.
10. The project possesses significant replication potential, both within the two participating countries and, also, in other countries. The project has successfully incorporated knowledge acquired from a previous project (i.e. Energising Development (EnDev)), and intends to replicate its good practices. However, the success of the proposed project will largely depend on strengthening the producers of ICS, as intended, ensuring the ability of last-mile entrepreneurs, promoting design improvements to address context specificity and longevity of the devices, and

<!-- image -->

- providing access to financing. The capacity-building efforts, as promised, need to be given due emphasis to achieve the delivery of the above-mentioned aspects of the project.
11. There is ample opportunity in the project for knowledge and learning. The technologies have already been field tested and handled by local actors, therefore, the project does not rely on imported and difficult knowhow. The advantage is further consolidated by the use of training, leading ICS producers to upgrade their production and credit-seeking abilities, and linking them up with credit institutions so that the supply side is significantly strengthened. There are knowledge and learning opportunities in the demand side strengthening efforts as well. Training sessions are designed for women's groups. Awareness-raising and access to financing are also considered for stimulating the demand side. Moreover, there will be targeted collaboration involving local academia (i.e. universities) to improve the design of ICS in terms of context-specificity, longevity and efficiency. Overall, the knowledge-sharing and learning potential are central to the implementation of the project.
12. The project also promises to contribute to the creation of an enabling environment so that further market penetration of ICS continues. To this end, the ICS-producing sector will be strengthened by means of: training; sharing of professionalization kits and other financial incentives; enhancing credit worthiness and access to credit support (and even subsidies); providing support in terms of research and development (for new designs); encouraging collaboration among stakeholders; and more. Finally, there will be support for addressing regulatory frameworks and policies so that the positive paradigm shift continues to pay dividends beyond the project lifetime.
13. By examining the opportunities that are likely to be created, as mentioned above, independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) finds the paradigm shift potential of the project to be high.

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

14. The project contributes directly to the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):
2. (a) SDG 7: ensure access to affordable, reliable and sustainable energy for all by removing supply-side barriers for ICS;
3. (b) SDG 3: ensure healthy lives and promote well-being by promoting household cooking stoves with greater burning (i.e. calorific) efficiency and reduced emissions of black carbon and fumes;
4. (c) SDG 5: empower women and girls by giving them greater access to a less healthhazardous and more cost-efficient cooking stove;
5. (d) SDG 12: ensure sustainable consumption and production patterns by creating reduced demand on forest-based, non-renewable fuelwood for cooking;
6. (e) SDG 13: take urgent action to combat climate change by reducing GHG emissions from fuelwood burning for cooking; and
7. (f) SDG 15: protect, restore and promote sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems and sustainably manage forests by saving woodlots from being consumed as fuelwood in inefficient cooking stoves in Kenya and Senegal.
15. Since the major effort will be to stimulate the production and marketing of ICS, the project will strengthen production and marketing capacities of ICS producing enterprises. It is expected that the supply-side activities in production and distribution/sales of ICS will generate 2,745 green jobs (supporting) in the two target countries. Moreover, the conservative estimates based on previous ICS promotions suggest that beyond the third month of the introduction of an

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.21/10/Add.05

Page 150

- ICS in a typical household, it will save fuel-related costs in households where people are habituated to purchase fuelwood from the market. In an average year, as high as EUR 70 could be saved per unit of ICS, which would be significant potential savings for a low-income household.
16. One of the major social co-benefits of the project is most likely to be realized through the enhancement of (primarily) women and children's health as they are generally exposed to indoor emissions of black carbon and fumes due to the inefficient burning of fuelwood. An ICS offers an improved burning condition where the calorific value is increased through the nearcomplete combustion of fuelwood, which releases lower volumes of unburnt/partially burnt biomass. This will improve the quality of air that the cook and children breathe. Not only will it reduce the number of people suffering from bronchial diseases, but it will also save costs on the treatment of patients.
17. The other major social benefit involves saving the time and effort devoted to the collection of firewood, especially in households where people rely on scavenging and cutting woodlots. Although a significantly high proportion of households rely on market-based sources of fuelwood, the time saved could produce many social benefits, particularly for women and children.
18. Any efforts towards the promotion of ICS have a built-in co-benefit regarding gender equity. Indeed, women in the end-user households will benefit most in terms of health cobenefits, time savings and even economic benefits. In the extension process, as many as 8,000 women's groups will be involved and educated through awareness-raising campaigns. Women will also be involved as extension service workers in the promotion of such efficient technologies. All of these aspects combined contribute to ensuring this is a truly gender-friendly project.
19. As stated above, under SDG 15, the positive externality of this project is to reduce the demand on fuelwood, giving the remaining forest resources in both Kenya and Senegal the opportunity to survive and thrive. However, without conducting a parallel forest management programme with sustainable woodlot management, following institutional certification and monitoring, achieving this goal in a meaningful way could be quite challenging. The project neither involves the relevant official institutions nor intends to engage in the larger challenge of sustainable forest management - the latter clearly being beyond the scope of the project.
20. Given the mandate of the project, it appears that it offers significantly high sustainable development potential.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

21. Both recipient countries belong to the African group, where the need for sustainable development is paramount and urgent. The Human Development Index ranking for Kenya and Senegal is 142 and 162, respectively, out of 188 countries listed, which clearly indicates the socio-economic challenges of these countries. Since the ultimate gain will be in terms of reduction of GHG emissions, and since the recipients' special needs to develop sustainably are well recognized under the UNFCCC, the project fits well within the financing framework of GCF.
22. The need for financial support for the recipient countries is well justified. Both countries exhibit the willingness to contribute to the global goal of reducing emissions and indicate potential in energy efficiency measures. However, the countries are not in a position to finance such activities. In 2017, Senegal's debt was 62 per cent of its gross domestic product, amounting to USD 16.37 billion. Similarly, Kenya's public debt in 2017 was EUR 37 billion, of which 51.9 per cent was foreign debt. The prevailing lack of financing prevents both countries from taking effective measures that would simultaneously address lack of basic and reliable energy services for their citizens and safeguard their remaining forest resources. There exists a critical financing

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.21/10/Add.05

Page 151

- gap, which the project may bridge. The majority of the financing outlined in the project is allocated to inject adequate financing to strengthen ICS production and marketing systems. The aim is to assist in the establishment of a business model that will no longer depend on occasional ODA servicing and will become sustainable enough to keep operating on its own. The injection of finance would not occur unless GCF allows the project to overcome financing barriers.
23. In both countries, the relevant institutions are found to be weak. The institutions need support to enhance their technical, financial and human resources capabilities. The overwhelming majority of ICS producers are unorganized, rely on artisanal skills with extremely low levels of financial means to respond to the needs of the market, and are outside the purview of formal credit support due to low turnover. 1 They therefore lack the resources to participate competitively in the market mechanism. Non-governmental organizations are raising awareness, as and when ODA-supported activities are made available. However, the entire effort could not even penetrate 5 per cent of the total market potential for ICS. Despite the existence of locally/regionally grounded academia and expertise, there has been limited research and development of ICS design that could meet the local criteria for an ideal ICS. In every facet of the institutional process involving ICS, a gap exists that needs to be addressed if the goals set out in the respective NDCs for the countries are to be achieved by 2030.
24. In view of the above discussion, it appears that the need of the recipients is high.

## 1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

25. The project aims and specific objectives are fully aligned with climate change and development priorities of both the countries. Both countries share a common vision regarding low carbon development - a fundamental aspiration under the Paris Agreement. As indicated earlier, the project outcomes will help achieve the targets highlighted in the respective NDCs. For Kenya, ICS market development is considered in the draft National Climate Change Action Plan 2018-2022. Both the National Climate Change Response Strategy (2010) and the National Climate Change Action Plan 2013 mention low carbon development, despite the fact that the primary goal in these documents has been adaptation. In Senegal, reducing the use of nonrenewable fuelwood is considered as one of the objectives in the NDC. It sets a target of the distribution of 8.4 million ICS units. The objective is fully aligned with a vision document titled Plan Senegal Emergent.
26. As the primary driver of the project, Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ) assumes both the responsibilities of being the accredited entity and also the major executing entity. GIZ is globally known for its relentless efforts towards promoting low-carbon energy technologies, which so far have been successful in providing more than 19.2 million people access to sustainable and climate-friendly energy services.
27. The project benefits from the experience of an earlier project called EnDev, which has been implemented and co-managed by GIZ. The project has helped target countries to achieve SDGs in a gender-friendly and climate-sensitive way. The EnDev project has exhibited good environmental and social benefits. GIZ has previous experience in working with relevant ministries in Kenya and organizations such as SNV Netherlands Development Organisation (SNV) and other non-governmental organizations. Although the project does not involve any government entity in Senegal as an executing entity, based on the past collaborations among GIZ, SNV and non-governmental organization partners, it may be inferred that the proposed project still finds a considerable institutional footing for its implementation.

1  In terms of monthly volume of production and subsequent sales.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.21/10/Add.05 Page 152

28. The project has been developed by involving the respective national designated authorities (NDA). The no-objection letters have been duly signed by the NDAs of both countries. Since human health is an important agenda of the project, the project collaborates with the Ministry of Health in Kenya. In both countries, civil society bodies have been consulted in the process of development of the proposal and particular attention has been given to engaging women's groups in the concept development stages. The latter has been manifested in the inclusion of over 8,000 women's groups in the delivery and awareness-raising around the project's subcomponents.
29. The proposal package contains a plan to further involve women in the implementation. It also presents a mechanism for grievance redress so that the implementation process occurs in harmony with societal requirements and sensitivities.
30. By examining various subcriteria of the investment framework of GCF, the iTAP infers that the country ownership of the project is high.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Moderate to High

31. The project is designed for two African countries: Senegal, one of the least developed countries, and Kenya, a low-income country. Incidentally, both the countries have high public debt, where a significant proportion of prevailing public debt is external and requires debt servicing. Despite the fact that multifaceted benefit potentials are available in the promotion of energy efficient and health saving ICS devices, such a project cannot be envisaged to be financed by non-ODA sources. GCF finance is therefore considered the best option given the nature of the project.
32. Out of an estimated budget of EUR 57.19 million, EUR 38.36 million is sought from a GCF grant. Since both African countries and their citizens, the majority of whom are poor, will be the target audience for a handy household technology that is also a money-spinner, the grant support is fully justified. A total of EUR 12.8 million will be co-financed by BMZ and a few project partners involving the two countries that include government agencies, project executing agencies and even the ICS producers. The consumers of ICS will purchase these stoves throughout the project duration at a cost of EUR 78.24 million. However, the latter is not considered to be part of the project financing. In spite of such an omission, the project cofinancing ratio is 0.503.
33. The majority of financing will go towards addressing the financing gap involving ICS producers. The ICS manufacturing sector, after receiving incentives and performance-based additional support, is expected to double ICS production within five years of the project duration and effectively supply about 14 million additional ICS units in the target countries' markets. 2  Given the budgetary spread as presented, the financing strategy appears to be innovative (e.g. the results-based incentive package) and justified.
34. The cost effectiveness of the project is slightly higher than other energy efficiency projects being approved and financed by GCF. However, a significant proportion of the cost is for training, awareness-building and social-engineering so that poor consumers are incentivized to purchase ICS units, which are generally costly. However, the implementation of the project promises to establish a business model. Unless the business-class producers populate the ICS landscape in the two countries and their capacities are improved and incentivized, they cannot operate at a scale that justifies the cause by attracting finance from market-based operators (i.e. banks and financial institutions). Since the business case will be completely market driven, which will perpetually serve the cause, even beyond the project lifetime, the financing is

2  With respect to the baseline flow of ICS units that generally penetrate the market in a business-as-usual scenario.

<!-- image -->

- therefore efficient in the longer run to ensure a transformative change in the cooking energy sector.
35. The project does not promise large-scale leverage of private investment in the project financial scheme. However, the project intends to upgrade ICS producers and help them become creditworthy 3  so that they can start attracting market-based financing for future scaling up and growth. Assuming the experiences of EnDev, the predecessor project, will be replicated and upscaled in the current project, the graduated ICS producers will leverage external finance on their own during or after the project period. Such an exit strategy looks viable and promising.
36. The financial and economic rates of return (FIRR and EIRR, respectively) of the project appear positive with GCF support. This indicates the project's financial viability. In both countries, the professional as well as artisanal ICS producers will have higher levels of FIRR and EIRR when the effect of GCF support is compared with no-GCF support scenarios. However, there are inter-country differences in financing return efficiency: the Kenyan average FIRR jumps from 5 to 2 per cent to 19 and 16 per cent for professional (i.e. graduated) and artisanal producers, respectively. For Senegal, professional producers are likely to incur better FIRR gains, from a negative 12 to 8 per cent, considering that GCF support is made available for the project.
37. Despite an encouraging financial outlook, the project gains will be sensitive to timing of rolling out upfront investments and the cost of various inputs. The current average prices for different fit-for-purpose designs of ICS are within surveyed estimates of consumers' willingness to pay in the two countries. If the cost of production and subsequent distribution increases, there is an element of risk in terms of negative FIRR, although the EIRR still gives positive results with a 10 per cent increase in capital costs and a simultaneous 10 per cent decline in sales, for both countries.
38. The project's implementation timeline is ambitious. It is anticipated that emission reductions will take place as early as 2020. It remains questionable whether mobilization of the artisanal producers with professionalization kits and subsequent training can be achieved so early that the new ICS units will start reducing emissions as envisaged. Much of the impacts and their effectiveness depend on how quickly the accredited entity, along with its implementation partners in both countries, can roll out and engage the current small-scale ICS producers. The effectiveness of emissions reduction will also depend on actual usage of the ICS sold, which needs to be brought under a strong monitoring programme. The project, however, promises strong monitoring for both ICS production, sales and periodic monitoring of actual usage of the ICS units.
39. Given the above analyses, the iTAP is of the opinion that, despite the risks mentioned above, the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project is moderate to high.

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

40. The iTAP recommends the project to the Board for approval.
41. The iTAP recommends the following to enhance the effectiveness of the project towards achieving low-carbon development in the cooking energy sub-sector in the two countries:
3. (a) Link ongoing projects that oversee forest conservation and restoration for the sustainable supply of forest products, including woody biomass; and

3  With a EUR 0.24 million grant, the project expects to leverage EUR 0.78 million in loan financing, which is only a small fraction of the total financing.

<!-- image -->

- (b) Deliver an awareness enhancement plan, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, involving the communities and fuelwood traders regarding sustainable consumption and forest restoration.

## Response of the accredited entity to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment (FP103)

Proposal name:

Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking: Kenya and Senegal

Accredited entity:

Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

## Impact potential

GIZ welcomes the positive review on the impact potential and ITAP's acknowledgement of the relevance of traditional and inefficient biomass-based cooking and its global impacts on GHG emissions as well as on health hazards. GIZ positively recognizes ITAP's assessment of the root causes of the sector's problems and the need for context sensitive market-based approaches in both Kenya and Senegal. GIZ concurs fully with the assessment of the replication and learning potential, and would like to emphasize the large potential for applying lessons learned in many countries globally, which still rely primarily on solid fuels for cooking.

## Paradigm shift potential

GIZ takes note of the positive review of the paradigm shift potential. GIZ concurs fully with ITAP's assessment that the key to the market transformation is moving from ODA-dependence towards a commercial market for affordable ICS that are well adapted to local needs .

## Sustainable development potential

GIZ welcomes the acknowledgment of the high sustainable development potential, and ITAP's recognition of the important co-benefits of the project for health, gender and the environment, in addition to the climate-change related benefits.

## Needs of the recipient

GIZ is glad to see the positive rating in this area and concurs with the assessment that the needs of the recipients in the two countries, Kenya and Senegal, are high. GIZ supports ITAP's view that there is a critical gap in addressing basic energy needs and the protection of forest resources, and that the project will make an important contribution to this area.

## Country ownership

GIZ takes note of the review's assessment of country ownership and ITAP's recognition that the project not only supports the countries' NDC targets, but also national agendas for health, the environment and gender.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

GIZ welcomes the positive assessment with regard to efficiency and effectiveness.

One clarification could be made to answer a question posed in the review: Emission reductions from expanded ICS sales in the first year of the project are expected due to two conditions specific to this project. First, the GCF project will scale-up existing baseline projects in the ICS

<!-- image -->

sector that are running now, and build on existing relationships with producers and stakeholders. Secondly, the project will start preparing producers and stakeholders for the coming activities soon after the board decision, so that the support activities for upscaling production will begin very quickly in 2020 and lead to notable sales increases within the first year. Otherwise, GIZ would concur that increased sales in the first year of an ICS promotion project could potentially be too ambitious.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

GIZ thanks ITAP for its recommendation to the Board for approval.

The project will take up the two recommendations regarding forestry and awareness raising for sustainable biomass supply chains, including to also link these topics to complementary activities of other ongoing as well as planned interventions by various implementers.

## Summary

In conformity with GCF's environmental and social policy dated March 2018, a Gender Assessment has been conducted for the project 'Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal', submitted by GIZ to the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The project consists in technical assistance and limited investments to scale up the current production of Improved Cook Stoves (ICS) in Kenya and Senegal, ensure product quality, support marketing activities, and develop awareness to augment the adoption of ICS by households, with the aim to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from domestic cooking.

The gender assessment shows that the project has major positive impacts on gender equality, by reducing household work for women and providing opportunities for women to engage in paid work in ICS production and distribution. The project has no potential negative gender impacts. Surveys of gender impacts of similar projects  were  available  and  extensive  consultations  have  been  held  with  women  organizations  in  both countries, to assess gender aspects of the programme. The improved cookstoves are unanimously accepted by women, and also by other household members. Regarding economic inclusion of women in the ICS value chain,  the  most  significant  outcome  of  the  consultations  is  that  women  feel  less  confident  than  men  to engage in entrepreneurship and to address administrations or other formal organizations, and less capable of writing and keeping records. This lack of skills and confidence hampers their access to finance, to available assistance programs, and to procurement opportunities.

A Gender Action Plan has been developed for the project, where specific training of women is an important component. Gender-sensitive monitoring is included in the project and should verify that women benefit from equal work opportunities, and equal incomes for equal work. The GAP is presented as a separate document.

The gender mainstreaming capacity of the proposed project organization is good, with several partners having significant experience in working with gender issues at grassroots level and in all areas of Senegal and Kenya. GIZ  gender  focal  points  will  be  established  for  each  country  and  area  of  intervention,  to  coordinate implementation of the Gender Action Plan. The project will improve the institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in Kenya and Senegal by disseminating knowledge on gender-related project experience.

The project benefits all age groups, especially children and the elderly, who are more likely to spend time in the kitchen. The comfort of ICS is also particularly important for persons with disabilities or with HIV. On the supply side, youth and women are mainly targeted in the project because (i) they are considered important agents of change, (ii) youth unemployment and lack of women's participation in the economy are two priority development issues in the targeted countries. However, ICS production and distribution are open to persons with a wide range of capabilities and ages, and the project value chain is inclusive of all groups in society, including the elderly because this will also help dissemination of the stoves among all user groups. The project also includes the adaptation of several workplaces in ICS production to people with special needs.

Both  willingness  and  ability  to  pay  for  the  cookstoves  is  good,  except  for  the  households  with  the  lowest revenues.  The  GCF  project  will  seek  further  opportunities  to  benefit  this  group,  by  collaborating  with  the relevant partners, such as existing projects with a specific focus on the very poor, so that this group can also benefit from the existence of a sustainable ICS sector with high quality and competitively priced stoves.

Domestic violence is a significant issue in both Senegal and Kenya. SNV and several implementing partners have experience in working at grassroots level around the issue of domestic conflicts and their experience will be essential for project implementation. Some stakeholders have mentioned the importance of ensuring that approaches to changing cooking methods in the household are always culturally appropriate when targeting new regions with different cultures. Activities to this end are included in the Gender Action Plan.

## 1 Introduction

The  gender  assessment  was  performed  during  proposal  development,  which  allowed  the  incorporation  of recommendations  for  gender  mainstreaming  into  project  activities.  At  the  time  of  writing  the  present document, the funding proposal was still under development. Some details and figures regarding the project description may have changed in the final Funding Proposal. 1

## 1.1 GCF project 'Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal'

## 1.1.1 General objectives

As  an  accredited  entity,  the  Deutsche  Gesellschaft  für  Internationale  Zusammenarbeit  (GIZ)  GmbH  is submitting a funding proposal for the Project 'Promotion of Climate-Friendly Cooking in Kenya and Senegal' to the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

The project aims at accelerating the growth of the market for Improved Cookstoves (ICS) and increase the share of ICS users amongst the rural and most vulnerable populations in Kenya and Senegal. It will enable these countries to significantly reduce consumption of non-renewable biomass in the energy cooking sector and reduce GHG emissions.

## 1.1.2 GHG emissions from the cooking sector and relevance for climate change mitigation

Burning solid fuels for cooking in open fires and traditional stoves releases emissions of carbon dioxide and short-lived climate forcers (SLCFs) like black and organic carbon (BC and OC) aerosols and methane (CH4). The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) estimated that replacing traditional open fires with more energy  efficient  improved  cookstoves  has  a  global  mitigation  potential  between  0.6  and  2.4  Gt  CO2eq/yr, while at the same time delivering a wide range of sustainable development benefits, such as reducing the pressure on forests and biodiversity, reducing exposure to smoke-related health hazards, reducing the burden on women and children for collecting fuelwood, and saving money for the poor (when the fuel needs to be purchased).  Global  patterns  in  population  growth,  urbanization,  and  historical  fuel  use  suggest  that  the number  of  people  relying  on  solid  fuels  for  cooking  and  heating  will  persist  at  a  level  of  about  3  billion primarily due to growing firewood consumption and rising charcoal use in Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA), which will counterbalance  the  declines  in  solid  fuel  use  in  Asia  and  Latin  America.  For  this  reason,  two  Sub-Saharan African countries, Kenya and Senegal, have been selected as the targeted countries for this GCF project.

## 1.1.3 Senegal

In Senegal, the use of wood-based fuels (firewood and charcoal) is the largest single contributor to the energy sector GHG emissions (66%). This is the result of continued population growth and the overwhelming reliance on  fuelwood  for  domestic  needs  (over  86%  of  the  rural  population).  The  population  is  growing  by  2.7% annually and the cooking fuel consumption by 3.1% per year. As a result, every year, about 40,000 ha of forest are lost because of the overexploitation of the forest resources. The UNFCCC estimates the fraction of nonrenewable biomass in total biomass consumption in Senegal at 85% (i.e. being used unsustainably and leading to net GHG emissions).

The Government of Senegal defined in its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) 2 significant  emission reduction targets for the energy sector. Because of the large share of emissions stemming from the use of

1 At the time of writing the Gender Assessment in September 2018, the project was still under development, but possible changes are not expected to affect the conclusions of the Assessment. The project description and project impact numbers have been updated in December 2018 and January 2019.

2 To the Paris Agreement

fuelwood  for  cooking,  the  NDC  defines  explicit  target,  i.e.  8.4  million  improved  cookstoves  (ICS)  sold cumulatively between 2010 and by 2030, and interventions for reducing the use of non-renewable wood as fuel  for  domestic  cooking,  such  as  (1)  Dissemination  of  improved  stoves  for  firewood  and  charcoal,  (2) Increased efficiency in charcoal production,  (3) Increased sustainable wood-fuel  production  and  (4) Diversification of household fuels.

## 1.1.4 Kenya

Over 80% of Kenyans rely on the traditional use of biomass as the primary source of energy for cooking and heating with firewood contributing 68.7% and charcoal 13.3% , while only a smaller proportion relies on LPG (12%), kerosene (11%) and electricity (0.4%) . The use of biomass is predominant in rural areas with about 95% of the households relying on it. Consequently, fuelwood consumption remains one of the major factors for  degradation  of  Kenya's  forest  resources.  According  to  UNFCCC,  92%  of  biomass  consumption  in  the country is categorized as non-renewable.

In its NDC, the Government of Kenya has committed to the mitigation contribution of 30% reduction (42.9 Mt CO2eq)  of  GHG  emission  by  2030  relative  to  the  BAU  scenario  of  143  Mt  CO2eq.  The  Kenyan  NDC  has identified ICS as a priority area for contributing towards the emission reduction targets under the category of energy demand sector. The specific NDC target for cooking energy is an annual abatement of 2.8 Mt CO2eq in 2030.

## 1.2 Baseline EnDev projects

The  project  builds  upon  successful  ICS  promotion  projects  which  are  being  implemented  by  the  EnDev 3 partnership  in  Kenya  and  Senegal.  In  these  so-called  'baseline'  projects,  EnDev  is  working  with  a  large network of local partners, institutions and NGOs, and has developed a good knowledge of the sector and an understanding of the needs of the targeted beneficiaries. The ambition of the proposed GCF project is to scale up, reinforce and geographically expand EnDev's current actions in the ICS sectors in Kenya and Senegal.

## 1.3 Household demand for ICS, ability to pay, and barriers to further ICS dissemination

There is empirical evidence that rural households aspire to own and use ICS. G. Bensch and J. Peters (2014:  Ruhr  Economic  Papers  #  494)  show  that,  among  households  who  were  newly  equipped  with woodfuel ICS, 95% were still using the ICS after one year, and 90% after two years. This drop was the result  of  the  limited  durability  of  ICS,  as  the  selected  villages  had  no  access  to  ICS  markets  for replacement.  These  figures,  as  well  as  the  results  from  the  user  consultations  held  during  project development, clearly confirm the aspiration of households for using ICS.

The ICS promoted by EnDev are well affordable for the majority of households, including female-headed households.  This  issue  is  discussed  in  the  Funding  Proposal  and  in  the  Environmental  and  Social Assessment. The payback period for the stoves is short, especially when taking fuel savings into account. Liquidity constraints can mostly be overcome by women entering a 'tontine' (in Senegal) and 'merry-go-

3 Energising Development (EnDev) is an energy access partnership currently financed by six donor countries: the Netherlands, Germany, Norway, United Kingdom, Switzerland and Sweden. EnDev promotes sustainable access to modern energy services that meet the needs of the poor - long lasting, affordable, and appreciated by users. EnDev works in 25 countries in Africa, Asia and Latin America. Since 2005, EnDev has taken a leading role at promoting access to Sustainable Energy for All. The Deutsche Gesellschaftfür Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) cooperates closely with the Netherlands Enterprise Agency (RVO) on the global programme level.

round'  (in  Kenya)  financing  system  that  helps  to  overcome  investment  barriers  of  their  members through group solidarity.

Therefore, the availability of ICS is a more important barrier to stove uptake than the affordability of the stoves. In the proposed intervention zones, there is currently no availability of ICS in most villages, with the exception of areas where the baseline project was active. This is why the focus of the project is on the creation of a sustainable market, able to grow without the support of development aid.

Available studies and consultations show that both willingness and ability to pay for the cookstoves is good, except for those households with the lowest revenues (see Funding Proposal and Environmental and Social Impact  Assessment).  Vulnerable  groups  will  be  supported  through  other  programs  and  NGOs  in  their access to ICS. The GCF project is seeking collaboration with many new stakeholders in the activity 2.2 (sector environment). Many of these stakeholders will be local NGOs and projects that are addressing the needs of specific target groups including support to vulnerable groups. These groups can then also benefit from the existence of a sustainable ICS sector with high quality and competitively priced stoves.

## 1.4 The ICS supply chain

An improved cooking stove (ICS) is defined by EnDev as a stove with fuel savings of at least 40% compared to the  fuel  consumption  of  baseline  stoves,  which  are  either  three-stone-fire  for  firewood  stoves,  and  Kenya Ceramic Jiko for charcoal stoves. In Kenya, the annual level of ICS sales estimated at the beginning of 2020 will be 240,000 stoves comprising of stoves for new consumers, but also stove replacements and stoves stacking. In Senegal, artisanal producers currently supply the market with about 200,000 ICS/year.

Stove production generally requires the making of a ceramic insert, made by potters, with a metal casing, made  by  smiths.  Some  stoves  (such  as  'rocket'  stoves  in  Kenya  and  'foyer  banco'  in  Senegal)  are  built directly  in  the  client's  kitchen.  Some  producers  are  specialized  in  ceramic  liners,  others  in  metalwork  and painting. Only a few larger producers perform both activities. The following types of enterprises exist in the ICS production chain:

-  individuals artisans producing only ceramic liners (potters)
-  individual artisans producing final ICS (smiths and/or potters)
-  individual builders/installers of stoves in the client's kitchen.
-  cooperative-like groups (mostly of women) producing only liners, but sometimes sub-contracting the metalwork, thereby becoming end producers of ICS;
-  larger 'workshops' producing final ICS, who may produce ceramic liners and/or buy them from other producers, depending on demand
-  industrial importers or manufacturers (only in Kenya).

Production is mostly artisanal.  Even the larger producers are merely large workshops, often without stone buildings. Mechanization is currently very limited. All categories of producers may employ workers part-time or full-time, on a casual basis or more permanently. In the current situation, ICS production takes place in the informal economic sector and workers have no contract. Unskilled workers perform heavy tasks such as clay and sand unloading and kneading with feet, and skilled workers have more permanent work in pottery or metal cladding.

ICS sales are either directly to the end client, or to a range of intermediaries such as wholesalers, traders on the weekly markets, projects or NGOs, women groups in villages. The term 'Last Mile Entrepreneur' (LME) generally covers sellers, retailers, as well as installers of stoves and providers of after-sales services.

## 1.5 Project outputs and sub-components

The project consists of two national-level outputs and 1 global output (excluding project management). The cost estimates below are those of September 2018:

-  Output 1:Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cook stoves in Kenya (GCF finance: ca. EUR 20 million; co-finance: ca. EUR 10 million)
-  Output 2: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cook stoves in Senegal (GCF finance: ca. EUR 18 million; co-finance: ca. EUR 8 million)
-  Output  3: Improved  knowledge  on  climate-friendly  cooking  solutions  and  their  contribution  to NDCs (GCF finance: ca. EUR 1 million; co-finance: ca. EUR 1.5 million)

## 1.5.1 Output 1: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cookstoves in Kenya

## a) Geographic coverage

The project will deepen the activities within the current focal areas (22 counties 4 )  of  EnDev Kenya, but also venture  into  seven  growth  counties,  where  initial  outreach  took  place,  and  eight  so-called  underserved counties 5 in the parts of Rift Valley, Central region, Coastal region and Northern Kenya.

## b) Sub-component 1: Strengthening ICS supply and delivery chain in Kenya

## Activity 1.1.1 Professionalization of ICS production

The project will upgrade about 52 local cookstove entrepreneurs from the artisanal level to the professional level,  and  18  from  the  professional  level  to  the  business  class  level.  The  project  will  also  support establishment of 20 new production centers in new target areas. Assistance will be provided on result-based

principles  in  the  form  of  so-called  'professionalization  kits',  including  technical  assistance,  materials  and equipment, and training.

The project will also support producers to achieve Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS) quality standards, and a number of universities will be engaged as Stove Innovation Centers (SIC).

Figure 2. Kenya: ICS Market Transformation Approach

<!-- image -->

## Activity 1.1.2 Expansion of distribution and retail channels

Distribution  will  be  enhanced  by  training  and  supporting  an  additional  2,700  last-mile  entrepreneurs, particularly in new areas.

## Activity 1.1.3 Facilitating access to market-based finance and other sources of capital

The project will facilitate access to market-based finance and other sources of capital, including:

- a) Capacity building for business class stove producers and distributers
- b) Stimulation and development of alternative financing and credit models for business class producers and  distributers,  including  establishment  of  SACCOs 6 ,  promotion  of  alternative  credit  facilities, integration of ICS finance into existing government funding platforms, and facilitation of access  to finance through interest rate subsidies or conditional output-based grants to financial institutions and other financial intermediaries.

## c) Sub-component 2: Enhancing consumer demand and ICS market environment in Kenya

## Activity 1.2.1 Raising consumer awareness

The awareness creation strategy and messaging will focus on elements of behavioral change. To this end, the project  will  collaborate  with,  and  support,  the  Clean  Cooking  Association  of  Kenya  (CCAK),  government

6 Savings and Credit Cooperatives (SACCOs) are community based financial institutions. A Sacco is owned, managed and run by its members who have  a  common  bond.  Membership  is  open  to  all  community  members  regardless  of  race,  tribe,  gender,  political  affiliation,  religion  or  job status. A member of the Sacco is a person admitted to membership after registration in accordance with the Sacco's by-laws.

agencies particularly, the Ministry of Energy, Ministry of Health and Ministry of Environment both at national and  county  level.  The  existing  networks  of  Community  Health  Volunteers  (CHVs),  Community  Forest Associations (CFAs), agricultural extension officers and Water Users Associations (WUAs) and women groups among others, will be the main channels for these campaigns.

## Activity 1.2.2 Creation of enabling market environment

The GCF project implementers will work with the Ministry of Energy to improve the policy and regulatory framework for improved cook stoves, by supporting the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoE&amp;F) in strengthening  the  monitoring  of  NDC  targets  for  the  climate  friendly  cooking  sector,  and  the  Ministry  of Health (MoH) on indoor air quality guidelines. The project will finalize and facilitate enforcement of cooking sector regulations and standards by working closely with the Energy Regulatory Commission (ERC) and Kenya Bureau of Standards (KEBS)l. Technical assistance will be provided to strengthen stove testing capacities in existing stove testing centers, including the Kenya Industrial Research Institute (KIRDI). In addition, the project will support establishment of new stove testing centers preferably in academic and research institutions to complement  KIRDI.  Finally,  the  project  will  collaborate  with  existing  initiatives  to  develop  a  sustainable fuelwood industry.

## d) Sub-component 3: ICS market monitoring and impact evaluation in Kenya

The project will conduct regular monitoring of ICS market development and evaluation of project impacts.

## 1.5.2 Output 2: Accelerated market development for climate-friendly cook stoves in Senegal

## a) Geographic coverage

The project will extend into currently underserved regions, as shown in the map below.

Figure 3. Geographic project coverage in Senegal

## Expansionof theEnDevsupported ICSmarketinSenegal

<!-- image -->

## b) Sub-component 1: Strengthening ICS supply and delivery chain in Senegal

The project will increase the professionalization of the ICS sector and the transition of ICS producers to higher production levels.

## Activity 2.1.1 Professionalization of ICS production

The  project  will  provide  so-called  'professionalization  kits'  (see  description  under  activity  1.1.1)  to  ca.  57 artisanal producers (based on current performance data), and to up to 22 professional producers. They will receive  very  intensive  technical  and  business  training  and  the  technological  support  will  be  based  on individualized investment and business plans.

In addition, the project will support innovation and upgrade of ICS products, including revision of existing ICS design for faster production, developing and testing technical solutions to reduce exposure risks in commonly found  cooking  systems  of  Senegal,  as  well  as  conducting  lab-  and  field-testing  of  new  national  and international stove designs.

Figure 4. Senegal: ICS Market transformation approach

<!-- image -->

## Activity 2.1.2 Expansion of distribution and retail channels

The project will address deficiencies in distribution and retail network with a particular focus on six underserved  regions  with  lowest  sales  level.  The  project  will  promote  collaboration  with  existing  distribution infrastructure  of  other  sectors  and  facilitate  linkage  between  potential  retailers  in  the  regions  and  the producers  and  distributers  of  ICS.  Various  distribution  models  including  direct  sales,  third  party  dealerdistributor networks, micro-franchisees, social sector partners, and institutional sales will be supported. The project will select 650 young people in rural settlements to start retailing ICS in their communities as LMEs. The project will intensify the collaboration with women associations to better reach communities.

## Activity 2.1.3 Facilitating access to market-based finance and other sources of capital

EnDev  will  work  with  the  business  class  producers  to  overcome  'access  to  finance'  barriers  through individualised training and coaching of the producers. Micro-Finance Institutes and banks will be familiarised with  the  business  and  investment  potential  of  the  ICS  sector  based  on  documents,  meetings  with  ICS producers  as  well  as  site  visits  to  business  class  producers.  In  a  third  step,  business  class  producers  with qualifying loan application documents will be supported to acquire a loan from the MFIs/banks through an incentive mechanism. EnDev has met with several commercial banks and micro-finance institution to discuss interests  in  the  sector;  exact  terms  and  conditions  will  be  determined  further  during  the  implementation phase.

## c) Sub-component 2: Enhancing consumer demand and ICS market environment in Senegal

## Activity 2.2.1 Awareness raising of consumers

In Senegal the project will focus on expanding the market of informed consumers into the remaining 6 regions in the South and East of the country. It will establish partnerships for this purpose with existing services and projects working on the ground. These partners will also come from outside the energy sector, such as in the sector  of  health  or  food  and  nutrition  security.  The  project  will  intensify  the  collaboration  with  women associations.

## Activity 2.2.2 Creation of enabling market environment

The project will work closely with relevant national authorities to ensure regular exchange of information, support the development and application of a joint monitoring system, and support the development of an investment  plan  for  the  domestic  energy  sector.  Innovation  sharing  and  joined  learning  events  will  be organized by the project, inter alia, to promote sharing of knowledge on gender mainstreaming experience with relevant national and local institutions. The project will encourage parallel initiatives to develop a more sustainable fuelwood industry, in particular by sharing data and knowledge.

## d) Sub-component 3: ICS market monitoring and impact evaluation

The project will conduct regular monitoring of ICS market development and evaluation of project impacts.

## 1.5.3 Output 3: Improved knowledge on climate-friendly cooking solutions and their contribution to NDCs

The global output 3 will use the results, knowledge and experience generated through the Outputs 1 and 2, as well  as  EnDev/GIZ  global  network  of  partners,  to  improve  understanding  of  ICS  sector  transformation strategies  and  the  contribution  such  strategies  can  make  to  NDCs.  The  project  supports  the  following activities:

-  Activity 3.1 Collection and codification of knowledge on ICS market development from pilot countries
-  Activity 3.2 Facilitating knowledge sharing.

## 1.6 Direct project impact

The  project  will  result  in  an  approximate  cumulative  total  of  1,919,000  additional  ICS  sales  in  Kenya  and 950,000 in Senegal during the 5-year project duration. ICS sales will continue to increase after the end of the project. This will result in GHG emission reductions estimated 6.76 million tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO2eq) during the project period 2020-2024, and an additional 25.6 (MtCO2eq) in the project influence period until  2030.  The  project  will  directly  benefit  11.2  million  people,  including  1.91  million  mainly    rural households, 0.605 million women-headed households and 5.57 million children. It will also limit consumption of non-renewable biomass in the cooking sector.

## 1.7 Project employment impact

The project is expected to create an estimated 2,745 work opportunities by the project end, including about 705 jobs in ICS production sector alone and the rest along the ICS distribution/sales chain.

In Kenya, the number of jobs in the supported production sector is expected to increase by 360 by the end of the  project.  Besides,  2,700  installers  and  retailers  (last-mile  entrepreneurs)  will  be  trained  and  it  is conservatively assumed that at least two thirds of them will be involved in ICS sales/distribution. Therefore, the project is expected to create about 1,980 work opportunities in total in Kenya.

In Senegal, the number of jobs in the supported production sector is expected to increase by 345 at the end of the project. Besides, 700 installers and retailers (last-mile entrepreneurs) will be trained. Additionally, women groups will be supported for selling ICS. The project is expected to create about 765 work opportunities in the country.

## 1.8 Project gender dimension and impact

The project  has  a  very  strong  gender  dimension.  The  cooking  energy  sector  is  a  sector  where  women  are traditionally the main agents of change, and the sustainability of the project will depend for a great part on how their needs and capacities can be harnessed. The project particularly benefits women and children by improving indoor air quality, and reducing time and efforts spent on collecting fuel. The project also provides work,  income  generation  and  capacity  building  opportunities  for  women,  and  the  existing  EnDev  baseline projects have been shown to have a positive impact on empowerment of women in both Kenya and Senegal.

## 1.9 Purpose of the gender assessment and Gender Action Plan

## 1.9.1 GIZ approach for gender mainstreaming

Gender  equality  is  an  essential  condition  for  human-rights-based,  economically  and  environmentally sustainable  and  socially  just  development.  It  is  an  objective  in  itself  and  a  mandatory  guiding  principle  of international and national (development) policy and cooperation.

GIZ's Safeguards and Gender Management System and Gender Strategy require a gender assessment in the preparation phase of the project in order to identify potentials for promoting gender equality and identify risks that need to be prevented or mitigated through specific measures. The results and recommendations of this assessment are taken into account for the objectives, indicators and the results monitoring system of the project.

Despite existing legal principles and binding political commitments, gender equality has not yet been achieved in  any  country  in  the  world.  Women  and  girls,  who  generally  make  up  half  of  the  population  of  partner countries, tend to be affected by disadvantage and discrimination more frequently and to a greater extent than men and boys. When gender-based discrimination exists in the country, region or sector in which the project is implemented, positive impacts will be difficult or impossible to achieve and sustain. In the worst case a project can exacerbate the situation. Therefore, prevention through the application of specific gendersensitive measures (safeguards) is necessary.

To achieve positive and sustainable results, it is particularly important to actively promote the achievement of gender equality  and  women's  rights.  In  the  area  of  gender  equality,  the  Safeguards+Gender  Management System  therefore  goes  beyond  the  risk  assessment  in  the  sense  of  a  do-no-harm  approach  and  requires additionally, as a binding standard, the assessment of potentials for promoting gender equality.

## 1.9.2 GCF Gender Action Plan

The Green Climate Fund has decided to mainstream gender perspectives from the outset of its operations as an essential decision-making element for the deployment of its resources. Its commitment to gender equality centers on gender-responsive climate action programmes and projects that benefit women and men. Gender mainstreaming is central to the GCF's objectives and guiding principles, including through engaging women and  men  of  all  ages  as  stakeholders  in  the  design,  development  and  implementation  of  strategies  and activities to be financed (GCF website, 2018).

## 1.9.3 Contents of the assessment

In conformity with the GCF's Gender Policy, the project has undergone a gender assessment to:

-  Determine how the project can respond to the needs of women and men in view of the specific climate change issue to be addressed;
-  Identify the drivers of change and the gender dynamics in order to achieve the project adaptation or mitigation goals;
-  Identify and design the specific gender elements to be included in the project activities;
-  Estimate the implementation budgets;
-  Select output, outcome and impact indicators; and
-  Design project implementation and monitoring institutional arrangements.

Following this assessment, a Gender Action Plan is developed which is presented as a separate document. The gender assessment has been developed in July 2018 on the basis of information from EnDev gender impacts studies  and  surveys  in  Kenya  and  Senegal,  complemented  by  consultations  of  women  beneficiaries  and

producers  in  Kenya.  The  Gender  Action  Plan  has  been  developed  in  consultation  with  the  EnDev  country teams in Kenya and Senegal. It has benefitted from the results of continuous consultations carried out during project preparation in 2017-2018 by the country teams with national institutions and NGOs (see Annex 2) and from significant inputs by the gender specialists in the Kenya and Senegal country teams.

## 2 Gender equality situations in Kenya and Senegal

## 2.1 Macro level socio-economic indicators

UNDP's Human Development Report (HDR) 2016 7 ranks Kenya 146 out of 188 countries in the medium human development category, and Senegal 162 out of 188 in the low human development category.

The Gender Development Index (GDI) is the ratio of female to male Human Development Index (HDI). The 2016  HDR  states  that:  ' Women are  more  likely  than  men  to  suffer  from  low  human  development.  Many groups are disadvantaged, but the systemic deprivations of women relative to men deserve to be highlighted because women constitute half the world's population. The deprivations facing women are the most extreme barrier to global progress in human development. (…) d espite the fact that in all regions women have longer life expectancy than do men and the fact that in most regions the expected number of years of schooling for girls is similar to that for boys, women consistently have a lower HDI value than do men. Much of the variation in  HDI  between  women  and  men  is  due  to  lower  income  among  women  relative  to  men  and  to  lower educational attainment among women relative to men. Part of the variation in the HDI between men and women is generated by barriers to women working outside the home, to accessing education, to voicing their concerns in political arenas, to shaping policies and to  receiving the benefits of high-quality and accessible health care'. In 2015, Kenya had a GDI of 0.919 and Senegal of 0.886, which is relatively low 8 , see details in the table below.

As an indicator for political empowerment, the Human Development Report states that Senegal has 47% of women in parliament (in 2015), Kenya only 20% 9 .  The  World  Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap report ranks Senegal on world average for political empowerment, while Kenya scores low on this criterion.

Regarding health, the Global Gender Gap examines gender-differentiated health indicators, while the HDR concentrates on reproductive health. Gender-differentiated health indicators in both countries are acceptable,  and  even  good  for  Kenya.  However,  according  to  the  HDR,  Kenya  scores  low  on  reproductive health indicators, while Senegal scores slightly higher.

As a summary:

1. Both countries score clearly below world average on gender equality.
2. Kenya scores low on political empowerment, but Senegal reaches world average.

7 The 2016 report was used since it provides a convenient comparison across countries

This, added to the 47 women-only seats and half of the 12 nominees by political parties, will bring the women in the National Assembly to 76. Three women have been elected governors. Three other women were elected senators. The number of women elected members of county assemblies  has  also  increased  from  84  to  96  of  the  total  1450  (http://www.theeastafrican.co.ke/news/Womenelected-in-Kenya-/2558-4054988-d8a9guz/index.html, retrieved July 2018)

8 The largest differences captured by the GDI are in South Asia, where the HDI value for women is 17.8 percent lower than the HDI value for men, followed by the Arab States with a 14.4 percent difference and Sub-Saharan Africa with 12.3 percent

3. Kenya scores low on economic participation and opportunity, and Senegal scores very low. Female labour participation in Senegal is very low.
4. Kenya scores low on current educational attainment and Senegal very low, but the gap in expected years of education is reduced to about 10% in both countries.
