## 2.2 Gender equality situation in Kenya

Unless stated otherwise, most information in this section is from the OECD De velopment Centre's Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) country profiles , which provide comprehensive qualitative information on legal, cultural, and traditional laws and practices that discriminate against women  and girls (www.genderindex.org). Other reports on country gender situations which were available online often build upon the UNDP indicators already quoted above, or else were dating back to before 2010, and were rarely used. The SIGI often uses data from the USAID Demographic and Health Survey program (DHS).

10 Data for 2016 have been used as a comprehensive dataset is available for comparison across countries.

## 2.2.1 Background

Kenya's population is estimated at 48 million (about 9.6m households, 2016) and is growing at annual average of 2.7%. 78% of the population is in the rural regions and 22% in the urban area. 33% of urban dwellers and 51% in the rural areas (45% of the population) live under the poverty line on less than US $ 2 a day. Poverty is higher in the Northern and Coastal regions of the country. Kenya is characterised by a labour market that includes  household  based  enterprises,  subsistence  agriculture  and  a  small  wage  sector.  A  majority  of  the country's population is engaged in agriculture (32%) on family owned land while 7.7% percent have no work. Most people in rural areas (44%) work on family agricultural holdings with low or no pay (source: GIZ, GCF Concept Note.)

Kenya  is  a  multi-ethnic  society  with  42  ethnic  groups.  The  Kikuyu  are  mostly  located  in  Central  former Provinces; Luhyia in Western; Kalenjin, Turkana, Masai and Kikuyu in Rift Valley; Luo, Kisii and Kuria in Nyanza; Kamba and Meru in Eastern; Somali in North Eastern; and Miji Kenda and Arabs at the Coast. Most Kenyans are Christians (over 80%), Muslims are a minority at 11% (KNBS 2009 Census). Christians are distributed across the country while most Muslims reside at the Coast (60%), North Eastern (15%), and the upperpart of Eastern (10%)  regions.  Muslims  at  the  Coast  make  about  50%  of  the  entire  population  in  the  area  (CIA  Factbook, 2017).

## 2.2.2 Institutions and norms

## a) Overview

Characteristic for Kenya's institutional situation is the progressive 2010 Constitution which guarantees, among others, equal rights for men and women. The National Gender Equality Commission (NGEC) was established in August 2011. Its goal is to contribute to the reduction of gender inequalities and the discrimination against all; women,  men,  persons  with  disabilities,  the  youth,  children,  the  elderly,  minorities  and  marginalized communities. However, it remains difficult in Kenya to mainstream gender in practice. Customary law as well as the Muslim Sharia law are used in the country and may determine issues like marriage, parental authority or  inheritance,  with  usually  less  equal  provisions  than  in  the  law.  Contextual  issues  in  Kenya  which  may impacts gender equality are the high level of conflicts-sensitivity in the country, sometimes ethnically driven, the use of customary law, the poor reliability of the police forces, land disputes and forced displacement of indigenous populations related to the colonial past, and gender-based violence.

## b) Family code

The 2010 Kenyan Constitution gives equal rights to parties to a marriage between a woman and a man. The 2013 Marriage Bill gives equal legal recognition to all types of marriages, provides for a simplified procedure for matrimonial matter, and allows for the registration of marriages performed under customary law. The bill makes it much easier for married women to understand their rights.  Since April 2014, polygamy is legal in Kenya.

Parental authority is equally shared by men and women and there are no legal grounds to prevent married or unmarried  women  from  becoming  heads  of  household.  However,  Muslim  and  customary  marriages  are discriminatory in this matter. Custody is almost always awarded to the father when a customary marriage ends in divorce.

The Kenyan law provides equal inheritance rights to women and men. However, in practice, these rights are not applied equally. Divorced and separated women are vulnerable; both formal and informal court systems discriminate against women (adapted from OECD, 2014).

## c) Physical integrity

There is no specific law against domestic violence. According to the country's previous two reports to CEDAW, police are frequently reluctant to intervene in what they consider to be a 'family matter'. According to a study conducted by the Federation of Women Lawyers in Kenya, 'women are most often violated by their male relatives mainly as a result of the skewed power equation at the domestic level.' Almost half of Kenyans find intimate partner violence acceptable. Given five reasons why a man might be justified in beating his wife, 43.7%  of  men  and  52.6%  of  women  interviewed  for  the  2008/09  Demographic  and  Health  Survey  (DHS) agreed with at least one reason. Men were also less likely to support wife beating for each of the five reasons. Under the Sexual Offences Act (2006), all forms of sexual assault are criminalized. Under the Act, consent cannot  be  granted  by  a  disabled  person  or  a  child  under  18;  however,  marital  rape  is  not  specifically addressed, nor is it clear whether the perpetrator can escape prosecution for rape by marrying the victim.

A 2008/2008 household survey showed that 60% of women who have been married, divorced or separated have experienced violence. 12% of women aged 15-49 reported that their first sexual experience was forced against their will. 41% of women reported experience physical and/or sexual violence in their lifetime. The police and the justice system rarely prosecute cases of rape, and local women's rights organisations claim that police  reluctance  to  investigate  rape  cases,  and  procedures  in  handling  rape  cases  act  as  a  significant deterrent to reporting.

The culture of silence around gender-based violence makes both reporting acts of violence and collection of data on the matter challenging. There were widespread reports of rape and sexual assault during the postelection  violence  in  Kenya  in  2008  and  2017.  The  2010  report  of  the  Committee  on  the  Elimination  of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) states that much of the 2008 sexual violence was ethnically driven.

Sexual harassment is addressed under the Sexual Offences Act and in the Employment Law, but according to the US Department of State (2012), sexual harassment laws were not effectively enforced.

The law does not allow abortion, however doctors have been permitted to carry out abortions in cases where the woman's life was in danger. A Reproductive Health and Rights Bill, which would allow abortion in cases of rape, as well as safeguard the reproductive health of women and girls in Kenya, has been under consideration since 2010.

## d) Son bias

There is evidence to suggest that Kenya is a country of low concern in relation to missing women. The gender gap in pre-primary and primary education is very small, in detriment of girls. The trend is more significant in secondary  education.  Data  on  the  gender  division  of  household  chores  between  girls  and  boys  were  not available.  Based  on  2002-2012  data  from  UNICEF,  child  labour  slightly  affected  males  more  than  female children. Malnutrition data from 2009 indicates that male children were more vulnerable in this regard than females.

## e) Access to resources and assets

The Constitution guarantees equality of ownership rights for all Kenyan citizens. Women are free to buy, own and sell assets as they choose. However, in practice women's access to land and access to property other than land are severely restricted by customary law, which prohibits women from owning or inheriting land and other forms of property. Women in Kenya are granted 'life interest' in property rather than full ownership, which prevents them from using it as collateral for bank loans or from disposing of it as they see fit. In the event  of  her  husband's  death,  this  'interest'  disappears  upon  remarriage.  According  to  the  Food  and Agriculture Organisation (FAO), women are only 5% of registered landholders. Even when women are able to acquire assets in their own name, their husbands often act as intermediaries in the transaction.

There have been several recent advances in legislation addressing women's land rights. The National Land Policy  adopted in December 2009 calls for the government to repeal existing laws and outlaw regulations, customs and practices constituting discrimination against women and children in relation to land ownership. In 2012, three laws that aimed to harmonize the various land laws were adopted: Act No. 5 of 2012, National Land Commission Act; Act No. 6 of 2012, Land Act; and Act No. 3 of 2012: Land Registration Act.

The 2012 National Land Commission Act established a National Land Commission for the management of land in Kenya, in part to remove the current discriminatory practices against women in the formal and informal court setting.

Since  they  rarely  have  assets  of  their  own,  Kenyan  women  historically  have  had  trouble  providing  the collateral required by lending institutions, making it difficult for women to access financial services. According to  the  latest  report  to  CEDAW,  the  government  has  initiated  several  schemes  to  enable  women  to  obtain credit, including microcredit. According to the latest data from the World Bank, in 2011, 39.2% of women had accounts at formal financial institutions, compared to 45.6% of men, while the percentage of men who had loans from financial institutions in the past year - 12.8% -- was double that of women - 6.8%.61 However, women do make up over half (53.81%) of borrowers at microfinance institutions.

## f) Civil liberties

There do not appear to be any legal constraints on women's freedom of movement and access to public space in  Kenya.  Previously  existing  requirements  that  women  secure  their  husband's  or  father's  consent  before obtaining a passport have been removed. However, of the women interviewed for the 2008-2009 DHS, 26.3% reported that their husbands usually had the final say in deciding whether they were allowed to visit family and relatives. The freedom of movement is further contingent on class: according to Amnesty International's 2011 shadow report to CEDAW, forced evictions in Kenya's slums and informal settlements are common, and women are especially vulnerable to forced removals. Women have the same right as men to pass Kenyan citizenship onto their children.

Women and men enjoy the same rights to vote and stand for election in Kenya, although women standing for public office often face hostility. Kenyans indicate that they believe that men and women are equally capable as political leaders. Quotas have been legislated at the national and sub-national levels to encourage women's political participation.

Under  the  Employment  Act  of  2007,  female  employees  in  Kenya  are  entitled  to  three  months  of  paid maternity leave, and their employers are required to pay 100% of their wages during their leave. Fathers are entitled to two weeks paternity leave.

Source: https://www.genderindex.org/wp-content/uploads/files/datasheets/KE.pdf

## g) Other information

The National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) has produced a 2016 report which gives a very good overview of the equality situation in Kenya by means of extensive data collection and data surveys, as well as a well-written analysis, and which provided good background information. This report is interesting in that it provides data disaggregated per county in Kenya; unfortunately, the data are not presented in maps and a geographic analysis would require more time than was available.

The  role  of  the  NGEC  is  to  mainstream  the  needs  of  Special  Interest  Groups  (SIGs),  a  definition  which encompasses people with disabilities (the latter including people with sickness), children, youth (people aged 18-35 years), elders, and marginalized groups and communities. The latter, in practice, includes indigenous people as defined in international policy.

Homosexuality is considered illegal  in  the  Kenyan  law,  but  some  observers  state that  it  is  not  punished  in practice  anymore,  and  that  the  definition  of  'marginalized  groups'  for  the  NGEC  could  be  understood  as including homosexuals.

HIV has a high rate of prevalence in Kenya. EnDev Kenya has continuously carried out awareness raising on HIV in its program activities, during trainings and stove builder meetings.

## 2.3 Gender equality situation in Senegal

## 2.3.1 Background

Senegal is one of the world's least developed countries. The economy relies heavily on cash crops and fishing, both of which are vulnerable to climate change. Those living in urban areas have far better access to resources than those in rural areas; according to WFP11, a quarter of people living in the capital, Dakar, are poor,  compared  with  two-thirds  of  those  living  in  the  countryside.  Senegal  suffers  from persistently  high poverty rates, sitting at 46.7%. Overall, 17 percent of people are food insecure, and in some - mostly rural parts  of  the  country,  the  prevalence  of  global  acute  malnutrition  is  critical.  Rapid  population  growth (2,9%/year)  will  further  exacerbate  completion  for  and  pressure  on  natural  resources,  as  well  as vulnerability to climate change (source: GIZ, GCF Concept Note.)

Senegal  is  also  a  multi-ethnic  society.  The  majority  groups  (Peuls,  Toucouleurs  and  Sarakolés)  are  mostly Muslims. In Northern Senegal, Wolofs and Lébous are mostly Muslims, while  Sérères are mostly Catholics. Smaller ethnic groups live in the rainforest region in southern Senegal ('forest people'), near the borders with Mali  or  Guinea-Bissau.  Foreigners  or  «Nars»  mainly  from  Mauritania,  Algeria,  Morocco,  Gambia,  GuineaBissau and Cape Verde, Lebanon are mainly living in urban centers (Source: Laval University, http://www.axl.cefan.ulaval.ca/afrique/senegal.htm).

## 2.3.2 Institutions and norms

## a) Overview

Senegal's 2001 constitution guarantees equality between women and men. A National Strategy for Gender Equality and Equity has been developed, to run from 2005-2015. The country has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women (in 1985), and the Optional Protocol on violence against women (in 2000). Customary law and personal law are not recognized as valid sources of law under the Senegalese Constitution, but customary practices still exist. The Family Code grants most rights solely to fathers.

The  SIGI  reports  that  in  families  touched  by  poverty,  women  and  girls  are  disproportionately  affected  by gender  inequality,  with  girls  leaving  school  earlier  so  that  their  brothers  can  continue  their  education. Discriminatory practices, especially in the domains of the family and inheritance, persist, although Senegal has succeeded in putting in place a legal framework that, de jure, does much to protect women's bodily integrity. While  in  urban  areas  laws  protecting  women  are  generally  respected,  rural  areas  are  still  dominated  by customary and religious practices, and few women are aware of the legal rights that are in place to protect them.

The constitution and law provide for both freedom of assembly and association, although the government has at times restricted the former. Protests staged in the run-up to the presidential election in 2012 were broken up using violent methods, resulting in the injury and/or arrest of many protestors, and the deaths of nine

11 www1.wfp.org/countries/senegal

people. Public permits for civil society and political demonstrations were regularly denied or given delayed responses.

Freedom of expression is protected by the Constitution and is generally respected. However, journalists have been targeted for criticising  of  the  government  and  in months  preceding  the 2012  presidential  election,  a variety  of  incidents  in  which  journalists  (both  male  and  female)  were  harassed,  threatened,  or  physically harmed were documented.

## b) Family code

The Family Code of 1973 provides a minimum age at marriage of 16 for women and 18 for men. The payment of  dowry  is  legal.  Although  forced  and  early  marriages  are  banned  in  the  family  code,  as  is  having  sexual relations  with  girls  under  the  age  of  13,  such  marriages  are  reportedly  quite  common.  The  2010-2011 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) reports that among women age 15-19, 24.8% were married, divorced or widowed.

Many marriages are customary or religious and thus are not registered. Registered marriages can be either monogamous or polygamous, in which case the husband may marry up to four women. According to the 2010-2011 DHS, 35% of women were in a polygamous union.

The Senegalese Family Code grants parental authority solely to the father, and women are unable to take legal responsibility for their children. The father is legally considered the head of the household, is responsible for administrative procedures and expenses affecting his children and household, and chooses the family's place  of  residence.  When  it  comes  to  decision-making,  husbands  also  primarily  make  choices  regarding important  household  purchases.  When  it  comes  to  decisions  regarding  the  use  of  women's  earnings, however,  primarily  women  themselves  do  the  deciding.  It  is  only  possible  for  a  woman  to  become  the recognized head of household if her husband formally renounces his authority in court. The proportion of households  headed  by  women  stood  at  25%,  as  of  2010-2011.  In  the  event  of  divorce,  the  issue  of  child custody is contingent on the type of marriage. For civil marriages, the spouses must obtain a judicial divorce (this type of divorce rarely occurs outside urban areas); the court often grants custody to the mother while requiring the father to participate in their upkeep.

There are two forms of inheritance recognized under the Senegalese system: civil law and Islamic Sharia law. While living, individuals must choose which system they prefer; the default regime is the civil system. Civil law inheritance is more favourable to widows and daughters, granting them the same rights as sons. It is this form of inheritance that is most common in urban areas. In rural areas, customary practices vary from region to region. In some cases, women are expected to marry the deceased husband's brother, or expected to become part of a son's household.

Repudiation, whereby a man can divorce his wife unilaterally and without warning, is explicitly prohibited by law  and  women are able to request a civil divorce  in the  same  way  as  a  man.  However, women may not remarry  until  a  period  of  300  days  has  passed  since  the  dissolution  of  the  previous  marriage.  In  Islamic marriages, the power to initiate divorce lies almost solely with the husband, who may divorce at will. In fact, notwithstanding the secular nature of Senegalese civil law, the most frequent cause of divorce is repudiation. Woman  who  request  divorce,  especially  in  customary  marriages,  may  be  required  to  reimburse  their husband's for the dowry paid upon married, unless the reason for divorce is due to the husband abandoning the household or if he is sick or impotent.

## c) Physical integrity

The revision of the 1999 Penal Code introduced a clause on domestic violence. Victims are, however, often reluctant to report cases - preferring to appeal to their families to help resolve the issue - and even when

they do, the police rarely intervene and few cases reach court.  There have been critiques of judges, who fail to adequately apply the domestic violence law. Accurate up-to-date data as to the prevalence of domestic violence  is  not  available,  although  data  on  attitudes  suggest  the  latter  is  widely  accepted.  The  2010-2011 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) reports that 60% of women agreed that her husband was justified in beating her for one of the proposed reasons. Rape is also criminalised under the 1999 revised penal code, but spousal rape is not recognized. Very few rape cases are taken to court, and of those that are, few result in a conviction; the most recent estimates from the Ministry of Justice report that in 2009, 47% of accused rapists went unpunished and were released without facing a trial. In part, this is because sexual violence and rape remain taboo topics and cases are often settled out of court.

Senegal's penal code was revised in January 1999 to include a clause specifically regarding sexual harassment. While prevalence data is not available, sexual harassment is reportedly common, and the law not effectively enforced.  Women's  rights  groups  have  lamented  the  fact  that  providing  sufficient  proof  to  secure prosecutions is difficult if not impossible and reports of sexual harassment by school staff are common.

Female  genital  mutilation  (FGM)  has  been  illegal  under  the  Senegalese  Penal  Code  since  1999.  It  is  still practised, however, and is more common among Muslims and several ethnic groups than among Christians. According to the 2010-2011 DHS, 25.7% of women aged 15-49 had undergone the procedure, but these rates range  broadly  across  region  and  ethnic  groups  (from  only  0.9%  among  the  Wolofs  to  82%  among  the Mandingues). On the one hand, this practice is often linked to acceptance, respectability and marriage for young women, making it more difficult for some  groups to abandon the practice. On the other hand, the 2010- 2011 DHS reports that only 16.6% of women feel the practice should continue, compared to 78.7% who this it should be abandoned. There also appears to be considerable political will to challenge and eradicate FGM:  in  addition  to  criminalising  the  practice,  the  government  has  worked  closely  with  national  and international NGOs on campaigns to encourage abandonment of FGM.

The DHS reports that 30% of women in a union declare having an unmet need for family planning (among all women, this percentage is 19.9%). The average age at first birth among women age 25-45 is 21. Abortion is only legal in cases where the pregnant woman's life is in danger.

## d) Son bias

There is evidence to suggest that Senegal is a country of no concern in relation to missing women. Infant and early  childhood  malnutrition  rates  are  slightly  higher  for  boys  than  for  girls,  as  are  infant  and  under-five mortality rates.

The high cost of education means that, in poorer families, girls' education is often sacrificed to enable their brothers  to  continue  schooling.  UNESCO  similarly  reports  that  although  gender  parity  has  been  achieve  in primary education, as level of education increases, girls' enrolment rates decrease. In 2009, only 27% of girls were enrolled in secondary education, and for every 100 boys, there were approximately 79 girls.

## e) Access to resources and assets

Until the Constitution of 2001, women were not allowed to own land in Senegal, but since 2001 women and men have equal property rights in regard to land ownership, and there have been government campaigns to encourage leaders in rural communities to ensure that this law is respected. Women are legally entitled to acquire and own land independently of their husband or male relatives, and to retain ownership and control over  their  own  property  after  marriage.  The  default  marital  property  regime  for  civil  marriages  in  the separation of property, and it is the originally owner who legally administers property during the marriage. However, as mentioned in the above Family Code section, men are legally head of the household, and as such have greater access to agricultural inputs and, more generally, land and other resources. In addition, where

the dowry system of marriage applies, any property, assets deposited at the bank, animals, etc. given to the woman upon marriage are to be handed over to her husband.

Land is allocated - at least on paper - through local administrative processes in Senegal that do not officially recognize  land  sales  or  bequests.  Land  instead  belongs  to  the  national  domain  and  local  land  committees attribute use rights. Until May 2010, women were not allowed to be members of the aforementioned land committees, and as such rarely received land through this allocation mechanism.

Customary  practices  relating  to  land  ownership  that  discriminate  against  women  are  specifically  banned under the constitution, and yet in practice continue to limit women's access to land.

Women and men have the same legal rights to bank accounts and bank loans, but in reality women often struggle to obtain loans: because women tend to have use, but not ownership, of land, they are unable to provide  the  security  needed  to  secure  loans.  Many  farming  cooperatives  do  not  recognize  women  as producers in their own right, meaning that they cannot access the credit and other services provided by these cooperatives.

As mentioned for Kenya under section 2.2.2e), women have however good access to loans from micro-finance institutions (source: EnDev team).

Relatively low levels of French literacy also complicate access to bank loans and credit; French is the official language of the country and yet only 1 or 2% of Senegalese women speak the latter (compared to 15-20% of men).

## f) Civil liberties

Regarding  access  to  public  space,  the  constitution  and  law  provide  for  freedom  of  movement.  However, married women do not have the right to choose where to live, as this right falls exclusively to their husbands. Survey data suggests that daily movements are constrained. According to the 2010-2011 Demographic and Health Survey (DHS), 52.9% of women reported that it is primarily their husbands who decide whether they can visit family and relatives. In addition, 39.9% of women agreed their husband is justified in beating them if they go out without telling him.

There  is  no  specific  data  on  discrimination  against  women's  associations.  Women's  rights  groups  are, however, very active in Senegal, leading vocal campaigns against police failure to prosecute perpetrators in cases of violence against women, as well as encouraging communities to abandon FGM.

Women in Senegal enjoy full political rights to vote and stand for election. Members of Senegalese political parties, women's organizations and NGOs have pushed for a gender quota in electoral lists. While a law on gender parity on candidate lists was passed in Parliament in 2007, it was later ruled as unconstitutional by the Constitutional Council. However, in May of 2010, the National Assembly adopted the Law on Equality of Men and Women in Electoral Lists, and in the 2012 elections that followed 64 women were elected as members of the 150-seat parliament (or 42.7% of seats), up from 33 after the previous election.

Concerning workplace rights, there are no laws in Senegal mandating non-discrimination based on gender in hiring, nor laws penalizing or preventing the dismissal of pregnant women. Employers must, however, grant women an equivalent position upon their return from maternity leave. Women in Senegal are entitled to 14 weeks' paid maternity leave financed by the government, but not many women work in the informal sector and are not entitled to such leave.

## g) Other information

Homosexuality  is  considered  illegal  in  Senegal.  The  punishment  for  same-sex  relations  is  1  to  5  years  of imprisonment, plus a fine 100000 to 1500000 francs CFA.

## 3 Gender in the ICS sector

## 3.1 Problem statement

The lack  of  energy  is  experienced  differently  by  men  and  women  and  entails  different  consequences.  The same applies for access to energy which impacts women and men in varying ways. In developing countries, wood, charcoal or animal and plant residues serve as main energy sources for cooking and heating. Women play  a  central  role  in  this  prevailing  energy  system  as  perpetual  traditional  divisions  of  labor  within  the household keep many women at home, which is why they are more severely affected by inadequate energy services.  However,  in  many  societies  women  and  men  also  have  differing  access  to  decision-making  and financial  resources  within  the  household:  'Women's  access  to  decision-making  within  the  household  and community is restricted, limiting their ability to influence processes and resource allocation on many issues including energy.' (EnDev - Kenya, 2013).

The 'conventional wisdom' in the energy sector is that women benefit from health impacts in the kitchen, from more comfort and safety when cooking, from time savings which potentially allow income generating activities, from direct income generation activities as builders and sellers of cookstoves, from increased safety when they do not need to go out to collect wood.

The objective of the present gender analysis was to collect data to confirm how these statements apply for beneficiaries in Kenya and Senegal, identify possible gender-related barriers to successful project implementation, and opportunities for further improving gender equity in the sector.

## 3.2 Overview of available gender surveys

Baseline  information  was  collected  from 3  surveys  that  collected  gender-disaggregated  data  from the existing EnDev cookstove projects, and included systematic interviews and data collection.

## 3.2.1 Gender survey prepared in 2013 for EnDev Senegal FASEN project

The 2013 EnDev survey for Senegal gathered baseline information for the EnDev component in the PERACOD program by conducting interviews. For the ICS component, interviews were conducted with women: 21 users of  ICS,  one  president  of  a  women's  group,  and  11  potters  producing  inserts  for  the  ICS  'Jambar'.  Further interviews  were  conducted  with  an  ICS  and  a  gender  expert.  The  survey  was  conducted  in  the Bassin Arachidier area of Senegal (EnDev Senegal, 2013).

## 3.2.2 Gender survey prepared in 2017 for EnDev Kenya

A detailed survey of 108 households and 177 LME's was conducted for EnDev Kenya in 2017. The table below shows the geographic distribution of respondents. 170 LME respondents were working in the stove business (the remaining were working in solar panels only), out of which 97 were female and 72 were male. It is not clear from the survey results if the interviewed LME's were engaged in production, marketing, customer sales or other activities along the chain, but from the responses it is obvious that many were engaged in production also.

The  FASEN  project,  which  is  the  current  baseline  cookstove  promotion  project  of  EnDev  in  Senegal, prepared a gender assessment and a gender action plan in 2017. The project supported 568 men and 375 women as producers or distributors.

## 3.3 Gender aspects of stove usage

The 2013 Senegal gender survey confirmed the health, comfort and time-saving benefits of cookstove usage, adding the following details:

- -cooking on an ICS saves clothes by avoiding them to get dirty
- -cooking in an ICS makes it easier to carry out other domestic activities
- -the time saved daily by cooking on an ICS and not collecting fuel is not used for income generating activities, but rather for other domestic work in the house, finishing work earlier in the evening, and recreational activities (visiting friends for instance)

## The 2017 Kenya gender survey noted that:

- -for  70%  of  respondents,  women  were  the  key  decision  makers  for  acquiring  stoves,  this  was acknowledged  both  by  male  and  female  household  respondents.  Whoever  was  the  key  decision

maker,  the  respondents  also  acknowledged  that  they  consulted  their  spouses  before  making  the decision to buy a stove.

- -despite  the  women  being  seen  as  key  decision  makers,  men  more  often  make  the  payments  for stoves, reportedly because they have access to capital (land) and hence are responsible for family spending.

Cookstove  users  consulted  for  this  assessment  in  Kenya  in  2018  confirmed  the  many  benefits  of  the stoves. Women expressed ideas and wishes about possible improvements of the stoves. The local EnDev teams regularly listens to such wishes and checks if the proposed ideas are favourable in terms of energy savings.

No gender-related negative impacts or barriers to stove dissemination were identified in the available gender studies and the consultations. In more than 10 years of project work, there were no observations on gender-specific barriers on access to ICS. None of the field studies including external evaluations have produced any evidence or hint for a specific problem of female-headed households.

In  Senegal,  village  based  women  groups  are  at  the  centre  of  the  proposed  dissemination  approach. Female-headed  households  are  known  for  their  participation  in  women  groups,  as  they  require  the solidarity of the group as safety net. Hence, they will have equal chances to access to ICS compared to other group members and even better chances than non-members of the women-groups. Furthermore, both country programs will work with NGOs specialised on gender issues.

Some  limited  investigations  were  done  regarding  opportunities  to  changing  gender  roles  in  the household. The 2013 Senegal survey concluded that, regarding the sexual division of labor, it is essential to raise the awareness of men towards the hard work of women 12 . During the consultations held in 2018 in  Kenya,  it  appeared  that  men  do  not  significantly  change  their  share  in  household  duties  because cooking  is  made  easier,  however  one  man  admitted  he  had  started  to  cook  while  being  a  bachelor. Consulted  women  stove  users  did  not  express  a  willingness  for  these  roles  to  change  (but  consulted stove producers did, see below). In the Luo area where the consultations were held, the expression for 'being married' is equivalent to 'cooking for someone'.

## 3.4 Gender aspects of stove production

## 3.4.1 Gender organization in stove production

In both Kenya and Senegal, pottery is traditionally a women's skill. Women are mostly organized as women's groups of up to 40 persons (sometimes including a few men), who work as potters. All metal workers are men. In the larger private workshops, all business owners are men, which may employ between 5-25 casual

12 According to the study, this can be achieved through meetings and seminars that address gender issues or through activities that  sensitize  men  to  become  more  involved  in  the  work  traditionally  performed  by  women,  for  example  the  processing  of cereals  or  the  collection  of  firewood.  To  motivate  people  to  get  involved,  competitions  could  be  organized:  The  former  GIZ program ProCas organized a contest for couples in which the men who helped their wives the most in the rice production, were rewarded with bicycles, radios, cell phones and other items. However, this project has a focus on the transformation of ICS markets.  Looking  at  the  budget  constraints  for  'soft  interventions',  it  will  not  be  feasible  to  include  dedicated  gender interventions  at  village  level  to  follow-up  on  changes  in  the  division  of  household  labor  and  childcare  as  well  as  traditional gender  roles.  Considering  that  there  are  15,000  villages  in  Senegal,  for  example,  this  would  have  a  huge  additional  budget requirement for awareness raising. However, most of the NGOs that will participate in the project as EE or service provider do have a focus on gender work in their own profile. The GCF country projects will aim at leveraging gender support through these cooperation partners outside the GCF financed interventions.

workers. Most of these are men, some of whom work on the pottery and others on the metal. A few women also work in these workshops. In Kenya, one woman worked together with her husband who is a business owner, often managing the workshop for him.

The consulted private producers said they learned the pottery skill from their mothers. They also employed a few skilled older women to benefit from their experience. In Kenya, at least one woman is known to work as a blacksmith.

There  are  also  many  people  who  produce  stoves  independently,  often  next  to  another  activity  such  as farmers. These producers can be men as well as women.

According  to  EnDev,  the  income  of  men  and  women  in  production  often  differ,  because  they  perform different types of activities and do not spend the same amount of time on the work. The Kenya study showed (RWI, 2016), that 7.5 per cent of men earn more than EUR 270 per month, whereas only three per cent of the women earn that much. Conversely, half of the women earn less than EUR 45 per month, whereas only 28 per cent of men belong to this lowest income group. However, men usually work longer hours in their businesses while women instead are often occupied with household activities that do not create income. Especially in rural areas, building and selling stoves provides women with their own source of income and a possibility of more independence (EnDev, 2016)

## 3.4.2 Results from Senegal 2013 survey

In  the  city  of  Kaolack  in  Senegal,  two  women's  groups,  respectively  six  and  twelve  women  as  well  as  one blacksmith, are active in the production of ceramic inserts for the improved cooking stove Jambar. Out of eleven  women  interviewed,  ten  were  already  active  in  the  production  of  pottery  before  engaging  in  the production  of  ceramic  inserts.  Coming  from  traditional  blacksmith  or  potter  families,  women  value  the production  of  ceramic  inserts  due  to  its  profitability  and  with  the  support  of  EnDev  have  engaged  in  the production of ceramic inserts. The decision of producing ceramic inserts was taken by the women themselves (in eight cases), the husband (in one case) or another relative (in two cases). However, those women deciding themselves to engage in the production of ceramic inserts had to ask for their husbands' permissions, except for  one  woman  being  a  widow.  The  women  of  the  women's  group  Groupement  d'Intérêt  Économique  de femmes potières de Ngane Saër usually work from Monday to Saturday, whereas the women of the women's group Groupement d'Intérêt Économique Sara Ndougary are divided into two working teams, each consisting of six women who work in turns every second day. Working hours are organized in a flexible way with the onset of labor between 8 am and noon and the end of work between 3 pm and 6 pm. On average the daily working hours amount to 6.9 hours. Since the production of ceramic inserts is hard work (for example, the dry clay  is  pounded  manually),  in  nine  out  of  eleven  cases,  the  majority  of  the  potters'  domestic  chores  is assumed by a female relative, allowing the interviewees to rest after coming home from work. The other two interviewees bear the full burden of domestic chores themselves and perform their household tasks before or after  their  vocational  work.  Still,  seven  out  of  the  nine  interviewees  perform  domestic  chores  such  as  the preparation of meals, cleaning and other chores before going to work. One could therefore argue that the production  of  ceramic  inserts  increases  the  women's  workload  and  in  at  least  two  cases  extends  their workday.  However,  ten  out  of  eleven  potters  were  already  active  in  the  production  of  pottery  before becoming involved in the production of ceramic inserts, thus the increased workload is not directly related to the production of ceramic inserts but rather due to the sexual division of labor within Senegalese households. Moreover, in eight out of eleven cases, it was the decision of the woman herself to produce ceramic inserts and all interviewees asserted the importance for a woman to earn money.

A woman who earns money is highly valued in Senegalese society and especially the art of pottery is very appreciated  by  the  interviewee's  family  members.  To  perform  an  income  generating  activity  is  of  high

importance for the women interviewed. Nine out of eleven potters reported that in a Senegalese household, both the husband and wife have to contribute to the household's income. This is the women's definition of gender equality. However, the idea of a man doing housework is completely rejected.

Compared to the income of a blacksmith, a typical  man's  job,  potters,  a  typical  woman's  job,  earn  more. Altogether, blacksmiths have higher material costs and more competition on the market.

## 3.4.3 Results from 2017 Kenya survey

The study surveyed 170 entrepreneurs, of which 97 women and 72 men, without clearly stating how many were working in production. Unfortunately, the data on the roles of men and women in production were not very  clearly  reported,  and  it  was  also  not  clear  if  the  information  came  from  surveys.  Nor  did  the  study mention if producers were organized in groups, or individuals.

At production sites, women are a majority. In all visited sites (the name and location of sites is not mentioned in  the  methodology)  women constituted more than 85% of membership, except in Muranga. The roles of women are fetching water, mixing of clay and sand, sorting, loading of liners and inserts in kilns. Sometimes they engage youth on a casual basis and few men who are members of the group to do strenuous work like digging and carrying clay from distance areas.

84%  of  respondents  use  the  occasional  services  of  other  persons  in  stove  production.  Some  male  LME's sought services of outsiders, while women were comfortable working with LME's in their group.

Women prefer to work with fellow women than with men and youths.

In  production  centers  in  Lake  Victoria  and  Western  clusters  of  counties,  women  are  the  majority  because 'most of them love the pottery work' compare to the Central cluster where the majority are men (unclear if these data come from the survey itself).

There were no gender differences in record keeping. The illiterate members of the group were assisted by other members.

Men generally spend more time per week working on their stove business, because women need to attend to their family obligations. However, 10% of women entrepreneurs spend more than 50 hours per week on the business,  arguing  that  they  are  passionate  about  the  job.  One  of  the  female  respondents  has  become  an 'engineer'.

All LME respondents are satisfied with their job, without significant gender differences in the reason behind their satisfaction, which is mainly income generation.

One  woman  acknowledged  that  she  does  not  depend  on  her  husband  anymore  for  money.  Two  women acknowledged that stove construction is an achievement for them since the art of construction is often seen as a man's affair.

## 3.4.4 Interviews with women producers in Kenya in 2018

Consultations with a group of women producers in Kenya yielded mostly similar results as the other surveys, with the following additions or slight differences:

- -the group of 15 women were organized as independent producers working on the same spot and helping each other in turn;
- -this organization allowed the women to work more flexibly around household chores;
- -the heaviest tasks were performed by hiring young men;

- -the women did not mention having to ask for their husband's permission, which is consistent with the differences in the Kenyan and Senegalese family codes;
- -some interviewees said that their husbands sometimes cooked for them when they came home late from the stove production workshop.
- -consulted producers estimated that a blacksmith would earn more than a potter.

## 3.4.5 Gender aspects of stove distribution

In Senegal, the FASEN project has supported implementation of a distribution network for ICS with 179 men (mostly kitchenware retailers) and 148 women (mostly promoters).

In Kenya, the 2017 survey of LME's showed that:

- -Customers buy stoves equally from women and men
- -Men  can  spend  more  time  than  women  selling  solar  products  out  of  the  house  (solar  products require night demonstration!) but this does not seem an issue for ICS
- -Attendance to meetings is good and does not depend on gender
- -Women  LMEs  have done more demonstrations than men  (solar LME's have done more demonstrations than ICS LME's)
- -Entrepreneurs in the solar business are sometimes required to travel and stay away overnight, which is  a  problem  for  women.  Interestingly,  the  survey  notes  that  some  husbands  have  allowed  their spouses to spend time away from the house after they have improved their sales.
- -Strategies  for  reaching  out  to  customers  differ  between  male  and  women  entrepreneurs,  women apparently using more phone calls, chama, and house visits
- -119 out of 170 LME's target women when selling stoves because they are the primary users, are more available  at  home,  and  sometimes  because  they  are  considered  as  being  able  to  convince  their spouses; 44 out of 170 target both genders.

## 3.5 Gender aspects of training

Training is an important component of the project. The 2017 Kenya survey showed that:

- -Women think that men do not like to be trained by a woman
- -Beneficiaries  of  training  were  satisfied  by  the  venue  of  the  stove  trainings,  which  are  generally conducted at ward or sub-county level, and easy to attend (this is not the case for solar trainings which require more traveling and are more difficult to attend for women)
- -Persons selected to train other LME's were also satisfied even if location was further, people enjoyed the prestige and allowance provided by EnDev was sufficient.

A  meeting  held  in  2018  with  individual  women  stove  builders  in  Kisumu  showed  that  women  still  lack confidence in entrepreneurship and are eager for receiving more dedicated training.

## 3.6 Assessment and conclusions

The assessment of the collected information is reported in section 5, 'Summary gender assessment'.

## 4 Gender in the sectoral institutions and implementing organizations

## 4.1 Project organization

## 4.1.1 Component 1: Kenya

The project Executing Entities (EE) in Kenya are the Ministry of Energy (MoE), SNV, and GIZ/EnDev Kenya. GIZ/EnDev Kenya implements activities at overall sector level:

## Sector coordination

-  Coordination of the Project Coordination Committee on regular basis
-  Consultation of the Project Advisory Board on regular basis
-  Coordination with Policy advice, development of policy documents
-  Contribution to the implementation of the stove regulation and the national stove standards
-  Coordination of the implementation of the national awareness campaigns
-  Institutionalization of stove training and awareness creation

## Professionalization of ICS producers

-  (Co-)Investment into production assets
-  (Co-)Investment into distribution assets
-  Linkage and capacity development to enable access to finance
-  Technical training on stove production and business development
-  Advise on work norms (social, health and environmental standards)
-  Coordinating Quality assurance, quality control, testing and branding
-  Extensive business training and coaching

The Ministry of Energy (MoE) has 16 energy centres throughout the country with 160 staff members. Since 2009, the MoE is the lead political partner of EnDev and is chairing the EnDev Project Steering Committee (PSC). MoE has the following experience in the ICS sector:

-  Provision of training of local artisans via the 16 energy centers
-  Development of the Kenyan ICS standard, coordinated by the Kenyan Bureau of Standards
-  Development of Regulations for improved cookstoves
-  MoE is the chair of the Interministerial Committee on clean cookstoves

The MoE has the following task in project implementation:

-  Chairing the Project Advisory Board of the project
-  Finalisation and implementation of policy and regulation via the 47 county governments
-  Development  (jointly  with  EnDev,  SNV,  Ministry  of  Health)  of  awareness  and  behaviour  change messages and strategy and coordination of the implementation via the counties governments and via the local associations
-  Implementation of awareness creation measures and stove demonstration/exhibition through their 16 energy centers
-  Contribution to training and capacity development ofstove producers and distributers
-  Provision  of  the  platform  for  coordination,  collaboration  and  knowledge  sharing between  the  two GCF projects of GIZ and WB.

SNV Development Organisation (the Netherlands) is a non-for-profit international development organization. SNV is working with GIZ EnDev program in Kenya since 2012. SNV is implementing the Sector Support project, aiming at coordinating the cooking sector of Kenya and strengthening the Clean Cooking Alliance of Kenya (CCAK).  SNV  has  established  collaboration  with  banks,  MFIs  and  other  financial  institutions  for  access  to finance. SNV will be responsible for:

-  Capacity building and results-based-financial incentives, access to finance
-  Support  awareness  creation  and  marketing  to  enhance  demand  in  cooperation  with  other  actors being specified in the implementation details.
-  Capacity  development  and  technical  as  well  as  business  trainings  along  the  supply  chain  for  the distributors of improved cook stoves

## 4.1.2 Component 2: Senegal

In Senegal, the EEs are GIZ/EnDev Senegal and several larger NGOs for regional interventions.

National level sector-wide interventions will be implemented by GIZ/EnDev Senegal as the leading EE of the project.  This  entails  all  activities  concerning  the  professionalization  of  the  ICS  production  and  large  scale distribution, as well as the measure to improve the coordination of the sector.

GIZ/EnDev Senegal has the following tasks (amongst others):

## Sector coordination:

-  Information of concerned ministries (including field visits)
-  Meetings of stakeholders of the ICS sector (including annual 'stove camp')
-  Policy advice, development of policy documents and relevant studies
-  Development and application of monitoring system for the ICS sector and its contribution to the NDC targets
-  National Media campaigns (TV, radio)

## Professionalization of ICS producers:

-  (Co-)Investment into production and distribution assets
-  Technical training on stove production, workshop organization
-  Improvements of stove design and production processes, materials and tooling
-  Advise on work norms (social and health standards)
-  Extensive business training and coaching
-  Linkage to finance sector (improve access to credit)

For reaching out to most of the 15,000 villages in the 14 regions of Senegal for awareness raising and last mile marketing support, the Senegal regions have been grouped into clusters of 2-4 regions. The project activities in  these clusters will be implemented by several NGOs with EE functions (Cluster Management Unit, CMU) and the GIZ. The EE have been selected together with the Ministry of Petroleum and Energy via a competitive Expression of Interest process, and have undergone due diligence:

-  ENDA ENERGIE exists since about 30 years in Senegal. Their work is focusing on access to energy, climate change, desertification and 'gender in energy'.
-  ENDA ECOPOP has 25 years of experiences in good governance in rural areas of Senegal. They work intensively with local authorities to improve the quality of their services to the communities.

-  CONCEPT  is  working  in  Senegal  since  more  than  20  years.  They  are  active  in  6  regions  for  the development  of  grassroots  initiatives,  the  strengthening  of  expertise,  and  the  recognition  and participation of stakeholders in the choice of development policies and projects.

The regional management units will implement, procure and supervise services for the following activities:

-  Awareness campaigns (cooking demonstrations, theatre play, local authorities etc.)
-  Local distribution (Last mile entrepreneur, weekly markets, women group)

## 4.1.3 General project governing structure

## Project Governing Board

The  project  uses  the  already  existing  EnDev  governing  board  structure  for  the  overall  governance  of  all activities. Members of the board are representatives of the Directorate-General for International Cooperation of the Dutch Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MFA NL), the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), the Norwegian Agency for Development Cooperation (NORAD), the UK Department for International  Development  (DFID),  the  Swiss  Agency  for  Development  and  Cooperation  (SDC),  and  the Swedish International Development Cooperation Agency (SIDA). Meetings take place twice per year. GIZ is invited to these meetings for regular reporting on project impacts, outcomes, outputs and implementation progress.

## Project Advisory Board (PAB)

The project uses the existing EnDev Project Advisory Board (PAB) for overall guidance and advice on project implementation  in  the  country.  In  Senegal,  this  committee  still  needs  to  be  formed  for  the  GCF  project. Members of the committees in both countries are representatives of the following institutions:

## 5 Summary gender assessment

The analysis of available documents, surveys, and interviews, yields an array of conclusions on genderrelated aspects which are relevant for the sector and project and will be used as a basis to develop the Gender Action Plan.

## a) Gender mainstreaming capacity in the sector

1. The  countries  and  energy  sectors  have  gender  policies,  targets  and  programs,  but  knowledge  and experiences of practical methods to implement gender mainstreaming are lacking.
2. The EnDev project teams have a very good track record of engaging with both genders at grassroots level.
3. Both EnDev country teams have staff which is trained and experienced in gender mainstreaming. GIZ headquarters also have trained and experienced staff in gender mainstreaming. However, among the non-specialist staff, knowledge on gender mainstreaming needs is variable. Experience with gendersensitive budgeting could be reinforced.
4. SNV, and potentially implementing NGOs such as JOYWO, have significant capacity in implementing gender projects at grassroots level. They can complement EnDev by bringing in significant experience in geographic areas not covered up to now.
5. There is a lack of gender-disaggregated data on current project impacts.

## b) Successes and opportunities

6. Women are the primary users of energy in the household and need to be involved to foster success of interventions in the sector.
7. Taking into account the needs of both males and females increases the rate of success and the longterm sustainability of projects in the energy sector.
8. The  baseline  projects  have  been  successful  in  providing  works  opportunities  for  women,  and  in empowering women.
9. Women entrepreneurs were found to be more efficient in selling cookstoves than their male peers when given the same level of training and support (Global Alliance for Clean Cook Stoves, quoted in EnDev Kenya 2017)
10. Women's  engagement  in  distribution  and  marketing  can  provide  other  women  with  comfortable spaces  within  which  to  discuss  their  particular  concerns  (Gill  et  Al.,  2012,  quoted  in  EnDev  Kenya 2017).
11. Women have ideas regarding design of cookstoves and are eager for technological innovations in the sector.
12. Women show a high capacity at collaborating and are successful in organizing women groups such as merry-go-rounds or 'tontines'.
13. Coosktove production includes heavy work (such as pounding and kneading of clay) which can be mechanized.
14. There are opportunities for work in the cookstove sector for people with disabilities.
15. Women are satisfied with the contents and convenience of the trainings provided by EnDev. They appreciate that these trainings can be performed near home.

16. Youth, the elderly and other Special Interest Groups, such as persons with disabilities should benefit from the project. First as beneficiaries who may be more involved in household cooking than other groups  and  will  thus  benefit  more  from  the  advantages,  such  as  reduced  time  associated  with collecting  wood  and  tending  the  stove,  and  the  reduced  exposure  to  smoke  and  harmful  air pollutants. Secondly, these groups may also find employment in the supply chains.
17. The poorest of the poor are not specifically targeted by the project, but it will seek opportunities with partners  to  involve  these  groups  wherever  possible,  to  allow  them  to  benefit  from  the  use  of  the stoves and or to be involved in the value chain.

## c) Potential negative gender-related impacts of the project

18. The available studies to date do not indicate that empowering women could generate conflicts in the household; most husbands on the contrary seem satisfied.
19. There are no indications to date that working conditions are worse for women than for men in the stove production sectors. The project's work opportunities in stove production and distribution are mainly in the informal sector.

## d) Potential gender-related barriers to successful project implementation

20. Women are responsible for 100% of household work. Domestic activities either prevent women from spending as much time as men on working out of the house, or force them to have a double workday.
21. Women who cannot perform their household tasks because they are engaged into stove production have these tasks performed by other women (mostly female relatives) 13
22. Women are often decision-makers regarding domestic purchases, but do not have access to capital.
23. Despite the positive results from the available EnDev studies and consultations, the country context analyses show that there is a risk that in some households, women will have a very poor decisionmaking power. This would be more pronounced in Senegal, in rural areas, and in 'frontier' counties of Kenya.
24. Gender equality in the household varies with class, ethnicity urban/rural areas, and per household 14 .
25. Women feel less confident than men to address administrations or other formal organizations, and less  capable  of  writing  and  keeping  records.  This  hampers  their  access  to  finance,  to  available assistance programs, and to procurement. Sometimes, women are less aware of why is it useful to keep records.

13 [EnDev  Senegal  2013]  argues  that  the  strategic  gender  needs  of  women  are  not  met  with  this  solution.  In Senegal,  by  generating  income  and  by  assuming  household  chores,  women  assume  the  role  of  the  woman designated and expected by society and themselves. From their point of view, not only productive but also strategic gender needs are satisfied even though  according to other concepts on gender relations  or  women's empowerment this would not be the case. In addition, the absence of the potters in the daily life of the household and the sexual division of domestic labor implies that it is always a female relative that replaces the potter with regards to household chores. While the production of ceramic inserts allows the interviewees to assume another role  in  the  household,  the  role  of  a  bread-winner  with  less  housework,  this  also  implies  that  another  female household member assumes the role of the housewife and the inherent household chores which are defined as women's work. In accordance to the definition of gender equality by the United Nations, such a system does not allow gender equality as said definition implies a society in which women and men enjoy the same opportunities, outcomes, rights and obligations in all domains of life (UNFPA n.a.).

14 It  is  important  to  observe  that  neither  the  consultations  nor  the  gender  studies  performed  for  the  project completely reflect the gender equality situation described in the overview sections of the present document.

26. Women literacy issues have been mentioned in Senegal.
27. Women tend to keep traditional roles in production (pottery), especially in Senegal.
28. There are indications that  men take on the most profitable positions in  the production sector,  for instance embracing pottery - a traditional women role - when this appears profitable, while women do not take on metalwork (only one case observed), which is at the end of the supply chain and has often (but not always) a higher added value.
29. There is a reluctance for husbands (and society) to let women travel and go out at night, and women fear going out. However, if this is an issue for distribution of solar panels in EnDev projects, it is not an issue for cookstove marketing and distribution, which can generally be performed near home.
30. Some men are reluctant to be trained by women.
31. Women are politically under-represented especially in Kenya, and do not have the same participation and decision-making powers as men. Their access to assets and lands is low, and their participation in the economy is lower than for men, especially in Senegal.
32. Access to formal finance is difficult for women (but women are more likely to be accepted from MFIs as clients because they are considered more reliable).
33. Gender-based violence is widespread in the countries and women do not always feel safe.

## 6 Development of the Gender Action Plan

## 6.1 Approach and methodology

The following approach has been followed for developing the Gender Action Plan:

-  understand GCF and GIZ requirements for a Gender Action Plan
-  based on these requirements and on the above gender assessment, identify needs for genderresponsive activities in the project
-  discuss the potential activities with the country teams and with local partners
-  select and budget the activities to be included in the project
-  define gender-sensitive targets and gender-sensitive monitoring and evaluation criteria
-  integrate the selected activities into the project logframe.

## 6.2 Rationale for the Gender Action Plan

## 6.2.1 GCF perspective

The GCF's commitment to gender equality centers on gender-responsive country programmes and initiatives that benefit everyone, women and men. This requires gender analysis to understand the social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated gender inequalities, and the potential contributions of women and men to  mitigating  and  adapting  to  climate  change,  and  building  climate  resilience.  It  further entails  adopting  methods  and  tools  to  promote  gender  equality  and  reduce  gender  disparities  in  climate funding by mainstreaming gender in the project cycle. Finally, it means measuring the outcomes and impacts of  project  activities  on  women's  and  men's  resilience  to  climate  change  through  gender-responsive monitoring and evaluation (M&amp;E). Gender mainstreaming should shape the entire project cycle, from project identification to M&amp;E.

## 6.2.2 GIZ approach

According to GIZ, the potential for promoting gender equality and women's rights must be leveraged wherever possible. This involves, among other things:

-  Creating equal access to law and justice;
-  Enhancing political participation, decision-making and representation of women;
-  Promoting equal social and economic participation;
-  Enabling equal access to education, to resources and services, to vocational training, to the labour market and to life-long learning;
-  Tackling gender-based violence against women and girls; and
-  Supporting state institutions and/or non-state stakeholders that actively campaign for gender equality.

## 6.2.3 EnDev Gender Action Plan

EnDev  has  developed  an  interesting  approach  which  is  relevant  to  the  energy  sector,  and  distinguishes between three specific types of gender impacts:

- -Energy beneficiaries -  Description and appraisal of gender specific reduced health and safety risks, co-benefits from the measure (job creation, etc) and inclusion of female-headed households.
- -Energy access accelerators - Harness specific roles of men and women as agents of change
- -Impact of interventions on changing gender roles (if applicable and feasible)

EnDev's approach takes into consideration the three types of impacts in its implementation.

## 6.3 Needs for gender-responsive activities in the project

The  present  assessment  has  tried  to  summarize  the  GCF  and  GIZ  requirements  into  a  set  of  objectives. Discussions have been held with the country teams about how these objectives should translate into gendersensitive activities to be included for the project. The results are summarized in Table 10. .

## Objective 1

First, the project should make sure to prevent any negative impact of the interventions on gender equality and any negative impact on any group of people. The only potential negative gender-related impact of the project is a very exceptional risk for conflicts in the household. In general, prevention of unintended negative gender-related  impacts  will  thus  mainly  be  based  on  regular  monitoring  and  on  the  implementation  of  a project grievance mechanism. It is also recommended:

- -to sufficiently tackle the experience of those project partners who have experience in supporting women in managing household relations;
- -if necessary, to carry out household survey of gender issues before intervening in areas where project experience is not though sufficient, in order to improve the understanding of cultural issues in these areas, especially when expanding into the border areas of Kenya.

## Objective 2

Second, the project has to  benefit men and women equally. Here the use of cookstoves in the household tends  to  benefit  women  more  than  men,  but  thereby  only  reducing  existing  inequalities.  The  further implication of this principle is that the project should seek to make sure that work opportunities as well as income generation opportunities are equally allocated to men and women. To this end, a series of barriers

identified  in  the  gender  assessment  has  to  be  overcome,  and  gender-sensitive  monitoring  has  to  be implemented.

One  barrier  very  clearly  came  forward  from  the  gender  assessment,  which  is  the  lower  confidence  and capacity  of  women  in  accessing  finance,  procurement  and  other  support  opportunities.  This  barrier  is identified by available reports, by EnDev gender practitioners, as well as by the consulted women themselves, and the project will contain an important set of measures to help overcome it.

Another barrier for women to tackle income generating opportunities is the social requirement for women to perform 100% of household activities. This subject is more controversial among gender specialists and is not necessarily identified as a barrier by the women themselves. It is discussed under 'other objectives' below.

Finally, there are a few indications that men may tend to trust the most lucrative positions in the cookstove supply sector, but there are no data available yet to support these suspicions. The project will therefore need to implement gender-sensitive monitoring of income generation in the sector.

## Objective 3

Third, differentiated needs of men and women need to be taken into account to ensure the efficiency and sustainability  of  the  interventions.  To  this  end,  the  project  will  engage  in  continuous  gender-specific consultations of project beneficiaries and translate the results of these consultations in project design.

## Objective 4

Fourth, the project should harness specific roles of men and women as agents of change, whereby women can exert a specific role in becoming 'energy access accelerators'. The baseline projects already have a strong tradition  of  working  with  women  groups;  this  has  proven  successful  in  the  energy  sector  and  will  be continued.

## Objective 5

Fifth, the project will include activities for capacity building in gender mainstreaming targeted at GIZ, SNV and the local implementing entities. Gender implementation capacity seems good in the network of implementing entities, but coordination and monitoring will be required since the number or partners and the geographical extension is large. Therefore a gender focal person will be responsible for coordinating activities, as well as for regularly assessing the capacity of  existing  or  new  project  partners.  A  training  program  in  gender mainstreaming will be developed each year for the entire organization. This program may include knowledge transfer from the more experienced project partners to the less experienced.

## Objective 6

Sixth, the project has looked at possibilities to support improvement of the national institutional frameworks for gender mainstreaming. The needs assessment identified that institutions generally have gender policies and  frameworks,  but  that  concrete  experience  in  gender  mainstreaming  and  gender  budgeting  was sometimes missing. The project, which has a strong gender dimension, a great number of beneficiaries and a large  geographic  coverage,  provides  an  excellent  opportunity  for  collection  of  gender-sensitive  data  and dissemination  of  lessons  learnt.  Therefore  knowledge  sharing  activities  have  been  included  in  the  Gender Action Plan, at national as well as regional/county level.

## Other objectives

The Gender Action Plan, in line with both GIZ and the GCF's policies, should harness the opportunities given by  the  project  for  improving  gender  equality.  This  can  also  be  understood  as  opportunities  for  better satisfying women's strategic needs. For this project, we mainly consider that the empowerment of women

through the project activities already described above (under objective 2) will contribute towards improving gender equality in the country. Following this discussion, this objective was merged with objective 2 in the Gender Action Plan.

Two  more  opportunities  were  identified  for  improving  gender  equality  through  the  project  and  were discussed with the project teams:

-  a discussion was held as to whether the project should tackle the gendered division of household labour and childcare. This subject is often controversial. There is no universally accepted opinion as to whether the solution to this issue is (i) to have men equally share the tasks, (ii) to provide public childcare or (iii) to allow women to work part-time so that they can combine household activities and paid work. There is even no consensus in the gender literature as to whether women and men should be given a choice among these three options, or if full parity should be forced. For the project, after consultation with the project teams, it was decided that the cookstove production sector provides opportunities for working part-time and near/at home, which were considered sufficient to tackle this need. The possibility of increasing the awareness of men on household chores in the framework of cookstove promotion was rejected, for fear that men may link cookstoves with the need to perform household chores, which would be detrimental for the marketing of the ICS. Only a component to convince men of the benefits of cookstove use by explaining how much time is spent by women on cooking was included in the project.
-  another discussion was held as to whether the project should foster its potential for changing traditional gender roles in stove production and in the rest of the sector. This topic is also a controversial one: some gender specialists consider it should be an ambition of every project, while others on the contrary consider that a sound gender-sensitive approach is to maintain existing gender roles and preferences. The decision for the project was not to encourage changes in gender roles per se, but to support every person wishing to work in non-traditional roles equally. For the project, this would mean support both men wishing to work in pottery and women wishing to work as blacksmiths; as well as women wishing to work as technicians, engineers, and trainers of men.

Finally, we also examined how and if the project should improve gender equality situation in the countries by working along GIZ's principles of:

1. Creating equal access to law and justice;
2. Enhancing political participation, decision-making and representation of women;
3. Promoting equal social and economic participation;
4. Enabling equal access to education, to resources and services, to vocational training, to the labour market and to life-long learning;
5. Tackling gender-based violence against women and girls; and
6. Supporting  state  institutions  and/or  non-state  stakeholders  that  actively  campaign  for  gender equality.

It was considered that the project, by training and empowering women, will contribute to improving gender equality along principles 1, 2, 3 and 4 above. Regarding principle 5, it is not considered a core goal of the project  to  target  gender  violence  and  sexual  harassment,  but  the  project's  consultation  strategy  and grievance mechanism can be used to help identifying and solving possible arising issues. Regarding principle 6, the project will support several gender organizations as partners, and will further improve dissemination of gender  knowledge  among  national  and  regional  institutions  (objective  6  of  the  action  plan).  Thereby,  the Project  and  the  Gender  Action  Plan  are  considered  able  to  successfully  contribute  to  GIZ's  gender mainstreaming principles.

## 6.4 Needs of other groups in society

Youth and women are mainly targeted in the project because (i) they are considered important agents of change,  (ii)  the  project  offers  significant  opportunities  to  improve  two  priority  development  issues  in  the targeted countries, which are youth unemployment and lack of women's participation in the economy - both GIZ and the GCF are committed to mainstreaming equality between men and women.

The elderly will also be considered as a Special Interest Group in the project. In country analysis, we see some evidence that the elderly are more likely to belong to the poorest of the poor, on the one-hand, but also more likely  to  be  employed  than  youth.  For  Kenya  for  example,  we  draw  on  the  2016  report  from  the  National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) "Status of Equality and Inclusion in Kenya". In Kenya, older members of society are considered by the NGEC as a "Special Interest Group" (SIG) as much as disabled persons, youth, marginalized groups and marginalized communities. The report states that older members of society are more likely  to  be  in  employment  than  the  other  groups  (youth  18-35  and  "working  age"  35-60).  Females  are disadvantaged in this group (as in the other groups) as they are less often in formal employment. The main issue for older persons is that they are more likely on average to suffer from poverty and/or hunger.

Older persons can be expected to benefit from the positive health impact and ease-of-use of ICS even more than other segments of population, as they may also spend more time in the kitchen and gathering fuel. Older people, who suffer from poverty more often than other age segments, will also benefit from the savings in fuel  cost.  If  they  are  economically  active,  they  have  the  same  opportunity  to  participate  in  the  ICS  supply chain as other groups.

ICS  production is already open to a wide-range of persons even with disabilities,  and the project will help optimize workshops to adapt them to people with special needs.

Targets for vulnerable persons (with HIV, with disabilities, or women-headed households) have further been included in the Gender Action Plan.

## 6.6 Gender-sensitive targets

The project Gender Action Plan searches to increase women participation in the economy, and the project has therefore  considered  setting  a  target  for  the  number  of  women  participating  in  the  cookstove  sector production and distribution.

The gender balance in the ICS sector is currently good. In Kenya, gender-disaggregated data were available on LME's, whereby 42% were women and 57% were men (EnDev Kenya, 2017), and on individual builders of rocket stoves (considered as LME's) whereby a little more than 50% were women (RWI, 2016). In Senegal, Table 5. on p.26 shows that men are still most numerous in the sector, because they are mostly the sole end producers of ICS (metalwork). However, there are more women builders of banco stoves, and gender balance in sales or in ceramics is about 50-50%.

The project will monitor employment of men and women in the sector, with the aim at obtaining a slightly higher  participation  for  women.  However,  in  the  market  approach,  the  project  cannot  directly  control  the number of women working in production of ICS. In the distribution sector also, the project does not directly support establishment of LME's in their activity, but only trains them. Once trained, it is their own decision to engage  in  market  activities.  Regarding  access  to  finance,  women  with  the  same  level  of  education  are expected  to  have  better  access  to  finance  than  men,  because  financial  institutions  are  thought  to  trust women more easily (source: country teams).

The project has therefore decided not to set gender targets for employment creation, but to provide more training to women in order to contribute to bridging the gap in economic participation:

-  by training more women than men: a target is set to train 60% women in each of the three training sectors of ICS production, ICS distribution, and access to finance

-  by providing specific training to women to increase their entrepreneurship and confidence.

The project will monitor gender-disaggregated employment creation, by counting job creating as explained above, and assessing gender distribution during the periodic surveys. The project will also monitor if men and women earn the same for similar work, and if there is a tendency for men to systematically occupy positions with a higher revenue.

## 6.7 Gender Action Plan, budget and M&amp;E

The  activities  are  budgeted  as  part  of  the  project activities. The gender-sensitive targets are integrated into the overall project logframe. The summary of the Gender Action Plan is presented below. The final Gender Action Plan and the project logframe are presented in separate documents.

## 6.8 Implementation arrangements

Implementation arrangements for the GAP have been included as follows in the project organization:

1. At national level, GIZ/ENDev will have persons who will be 'focal points' for gender mainstreaming;

2. The project will also have regional focal points for gender mainstreaming. In Senegal, these staff can be with EnDev or with the EE's; in Kenya, with the EnDev cluster management or with SNV;
3. All EEs and implementing partners will have designated contact persons for gender issues.
4. A project grievance mechanism is established:
4. o possible complaints  are  regularly collected by the EEs during their consultations of beneficiaries, producers, workers in the supply chain, and project staff;
5. o business-scale ICS producers with potential local impacts and nuisances will be required to have  their  own  local  mechanism,  for  instance  a  contact  phone  number  advertised  at  the factory entrance;
6. o complaints in writing will be accepted and treated by the EnDev country teams.

General E&amp;S management will comprise the following tasks and responsibilities:

1. GIZ's Head Office oversees compliance with GIZ Safeguards and Gender management system
2. The Project Advisory Board is responsible for overall guidance on the project, including on aspects related to E&amp;S and gender policies, and is informed of programme compliance with E&amp;S safeguards
3. The Project  Coordination Committees are responsible for monitoring implementation of the ESMP and the Gender Action Plan
4. The national gender focal person coordinates the Gender Action Plan across organizations
5. A  training  program  in  gender  mainstreaming  is  implemented  for  the  entire  project  organization, harnessing among others the capacity of EEs and implementing partners (suggestion: in year 1 and in year 3).
